FFALHS
51946
ADE-MDDIRE
The Australian Government has carefully consid-
ered the aide-memoires received from the State Department
on 26 September and 16 October, 1946, concerning the pro-
894.628/11-846
posed whaling expedition to the Antarctic from Japan.
The aide-memoire received on 26 September is
understood as conveying the following assurances:-
(I) That the proposed expedition is an emergency
measure confined to the coming season 1946/47
an
and in no way constitutes a precedent for the
future.
(II) That the Australian Government will be fully
consulted in connexion with any future proposals
concerning Japanese whaling with which the
CS/V
United States is concerned.
(III) The question of the future of the Japanese whaling
industry is a matter for Allied consultation and
decision.
(IV) That the ultimate disposition of Japenese whaling
equipment and facilities is a matter for Allied
consultation and decision.

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
(v) That the proposed expedition will be carried
out in full conformity with all international
conventions and regulations pertaining to
whaling.
That the expedition will not approach within
twelve miles of any land after leaving Japan.
(VII) Thet the expedition will be under full Allied
control and that SCAP will screen thoroughly
the Japanese crews.
(VIII) That all oil produced as a result of the exped-
ition will be subject to allocation by the Inter-
national Emergency Food Council.
While attaching much importance to these assurances.
the Australian Government is still most concerned at the
prospect of Japenese personnel being permitted to enter the
South Pacific and more especially waters adjacent to Australia
or Australian Antarctic territory. Although unable to agree
that the form of the proposed expedition is satisfactory, the
Australian Government has arranged to provide one inspector
for the expedition.
In regard to the second of the above assurances,
the Australian Government wishes to make it clear that it

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
would wish to be consulted on all aspects of future
Japanese whaling and not merely on those relating to security.
The Government is strongly of the opinion that the Japanese
should be prohibited from whaling in the Antarctic in
future in view of their past record in that industry. An
indication of United States Government support for this view
would do much to assuage concern felt by the Australian
Government in connexion with the present proposals and
would reinforce the first of the assurances mentioned above.
In connexion with the fourth of the above assur-
ances, it is noted that the SCAP's order of 23 August which
gave the Japanese Government permission to convert further
tanker into a whale processi ship remains in force. The
Australian Government would welcome an indication that the
United States Government would be prepared to support the
Australian Government's claim to receive a portion of the
Japanese whaling equipment as reparations.
In connexion with the sixth of the assurances
mentioned, the Australian Government would welcome infor-
mation about the route to be followed by the expedition
as soon as it is available.

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.97)
- 4 -
With regard to the second paragraph of the State
Department's aide memoire of 26 September, the Australian
Government desires to invite the attention of the United
States Government to the fact that the Supreme Commander
in issuing his order on whaling to the Japanese Government
on 23 August acted contrary to the provision contained in
section 5 of the Moscow Agreement concerning the establish-
ment of the Allied Council for Japan, namely, to consult
and advise with the Council in advance of the issuance
of orders on matters of substance the exigencies of the
situation permitting".

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
APO 500
AG 800.217(6 Ncv 46)NR
6 Noverter 1946
(SCAPIN 1321)
MEMORANDUM FOR: Imperial Japanese Government
THROUGH:
Central Liaison Office, Tokyo
SUBJECT:
Japanesc Whaling Expedition to the Antarctic Ocean
1. Reference Momorandum for the Imperial Japanose Government for
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers AG 8CO.217
(6 Aug 46)NR (SCAPIN 1103) 6 August 1946 subject as above.
2. The following officers of the United States Army will accompany
tho Japanese Whaling Fleet on its voyage to the Antarctic Ocean as repre-
sentatives of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers:
Captain William M. Terry. AUS, O-1825893, SCAP, Representative.
lst Lt David R. McCracken, TC, O-1950400, SCAP, Representative.
3. The above named officers will accompany the Japanese whaling fleet
to insure Japanese compliance with terms of the International Whaling
Conventions and thc provisions of SCAP directives relating to the whaling
industry.
4. Durin the period of the voyage, these representatives will be
acting under instructions of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
They will be given every assistance in accomplishing these instructions
and extcnded fullest consideration and courtesy.
5. Changes or additions to directives of the Supreme Commander which
aro required by exigencies of unforeseen conditions will be referred to
SCAP representatives for decision.
6. Necessary contact with nationals of other countries will be con-
ducted by the representatives of the Supremo Commander.
7. Radio commuinication facilities aboard the whaling vessels will be
available to SCAP representatives at all times.
FOR THE SUPRBME COMMANDER:
Colidnel, AGd.
Adjutant General.

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.98)
AIRGRAM SENT
Department of State
NO.A=159
Washington,
TIME
2242
To the
Nov 21, 1946
2242
United States Political Adviser to the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers,
Tokyo.
For your information following is text of note
received from Norwegian Embassy on October 30, 1946
on the subject of Japanese Antarctic whaling expedi-
tion:
"I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt of
the Acting Secretary's note of October 16, 1946, re-
garding the question of Japanese whaling in the
Antarctic under authority of the Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers.
"The Royal Norwegian Government understands from
your note that the United States Government finds it
necessary to uphold the decision to send out two whaling
expeditions from Japan for the coming season. Even
though my Governr ent has to acquiesce in this factual
situation, it is still of the opinion that it is un-
fortunate, from reasons previously advanced, that the
Japanese whaling expeditions have been authorized.
"When the Charge d'Affairs on September 6, 1946
presented an Aide-Memoire regarding certain British
proposals, he orally expressed my Government's support
of the representations made by the British Embassy
C
2
against the Supreme Commander's directive of August 23,
S
authorizing the conversion of a tanker into a whale
processing ship to be completed after the end of the
coming season. My Government upholds its strong
objection against the construction of a third Japanese
factory ship. It presumes that under no circumstances
will permission be given for Japanese whaling operations
in future seasons.
"The Norwegian

"The Norwegian Government will be glad to pro-
vide one Norwegian inspector for the expedition, and
intends to assign Lieutenant-Commander Harald Ostby
of the Royal Norwegian Naval Reserve. Lieutenant-
Commander Ostby was born February 2, 1907. He was a
whaling inspector for the inistry of Commerce from
1936 until the war, and since then has been employed
in the Whaling Division of that Ministry. It is
presumed that he will receive the same remuneration
and accomodation as the American and British
inspectors, and that the Supreme Commander will find
it possible to provide his subsistence. My Government
is prepared to pay his salary and expenses in the
first instance, but expects that these outlays will be
reimbursed out of the proceeds of the expedition.
"Accept, Excellency, the renewed assurances of
my highest consideration."

in a matter for allied consultation and decision.

FROM: Central Liaison Office, Tokyo.
SUBJECT : Whaling in the Antarctic Ocean.
C.L.0. No. 2416 (EA)
19 May 1946
1. The Japanese Government wishes to submit herewith to the General
Headquarters a plan for whaling in the Antarctic Ocean which it inten
to carry out as a measure for alleviating the critical food situation
in this country.
2. The plan is especially promising since Japan has much experience of
whaling in the Antarctic Ocean, and further, the plan has been formalate
on the basis of workable equipment and facilities.
2. It is deemed that materialization of this plan would go a long way to
improve the present food situation in Japan. It is requested, therefore.
that the General Headquarters be good enough to give approval to this
plan.
FOR THE PRESIDENT:
/=/ (s. Igucht)

MENDRANDOM FOR THE CHIEF OF STAFF.
I. THE PROBLEM PRESENTED.
To antherige two Japanose whaling factery shipe and la wha
or killer boats to conduct whaling eperatiens in the Aataretie daring
the 1946 - 1947 whaling seasom.
II. FACTS BEARING UPON THE PROBLEM.
1. In the years prior to 194l Japan was one of the chief par
ticipants in the whale fishery of the Antarctic. Since the cessatiem
of hostilities Japanese operations in this area have beem prehibited.
2. The Japanese operated six whaling factery ships of frem
10,000 to 20,000 gross tons each nd 45 whalers er killer beats in the
Antarctic during the l940 - 194l whaling season. During the war all the
factory ships and many of the whalers or killer boats were sunk.
3. The Japanese catch in the 1940 - 1941 season mounted te
9.857 whales that produced 100,939 metric tons of whale eil which found
a world market and appreximately 12,000 metric tons ef ether whale
producte most of which was frosen whale meat fer consumptiem as feed is
Japam. At present there is an acute world shortage of whale eil and
there is a need for whale meat for human food in Japan.
4. The Japanese Buresm of Fisheries states that, if permitted,
the Japanese can have two 10,000-ton factory ships, twelve killer beats
of around 350 tons each, and seven carrier vessels, three betweem 7,000
and 10,000 tons each and four around 1,000 tons each, ready fer the 1946
1947 season. The season normally commences in December. The whaling
fleets formerly left Japan for the Antarctic in November each year and
returned in April the following year.
5. With the equipment listed in paragraph 4, the Japamese
Burean of Pisheries estimates that spprosimately 2,000 whales coald be
captured and processed during the 1946 - 1947 season. These whales would
yield about 11,450 metric tons of whale oil, 1, 700 metric tons of fresem
whale meat, 14,352 metric tons of salted whale meat, 9,330 metric tons of
salted whale blubber and 12 metric tons of whale liver eil.
6. The whale oil would be made available fer allocation by the
Combined Food Board.

meat than weuld the whalers of ether matiens. tey have

8. On 3 November 1945 the Imperial Japamese Geverzment wee

the regulation of the International Whaling Conventions (scar  s00.?

11. The area in the Antarctic in which whaling eperatiens will
be conducted is shown on the enclosed map. (TAB 1)
III. CONCLUSIONS.
In view of the world shortage of fats, oils and pretein feeds.
it is desirable that the Supreme Commander anthorise Antaretie whaling
by. the Japanese during the 1946-1947 season.
IV. ACTION RECOMMENDED.
It is recommended that the attached memorandum to the Imperial
Japanese Government anthorising Japanese whaling in the Antarctic daring
the 1946-1947 season be sent forward. (TAB 2)

MEMORANDUM FOR: Imperial Japanese Government

SUBJECT:
Japanese Whaling Expedition to the Antarctic Ocoan
1. Reference Memorandum for the Imperial Japanese Government for
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers AG 8C0.217
(6 Aug 46)NR (SCAPIN 1103) 6 August 1946 subject as above.
2. The following officers of the United States Army will accompany
the Japanese Whaling Fleet on its voyage to the Antarctic Ocean as repre-
sentatives of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers:
Captain William M. Terry, AUS, O-1825893, SCAP, Representative.
lst Lt David R. McCracken. TC, O-1950400, SCAP. Representative.
3. The above named officers will accompany the Japanese whaling fleet
to insure Japanese compliance with terme of the International Whaling
Conventions and the provisions of SCAP directives relating to the whaling
industry.
4. During the period of the voyage, these representatives will be
acting under instructions of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
They will be given overy assistance in accomplishing these inetructions

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS

CHECK SHEET
Reply to Washington Radio Regardit
File No.:NR 800.217
Subject:
Japanese Antarctic Whaling Projeo
Note No.:
From: NR
To: DS
Date: 14 Nov 19
1.
1. Reference Washington Radio from WAR (WD SCA ES) to CInC
W 84976, dated 8 November 46. (Incl No. 1) Radio states in part
(1) "State Department requests information regarding qualificati
of American inspector accompanying expedition."
a. Two U.S. Army Officers, Capt William M. Terry and 1st
Lt David R. McCracken, are accompanying the Japanese Antarctio
ing expedition as representative of the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers. The SCAP representatives are not rep/eat not refor
red to as inspectors. The Japanese Bureau of Fisheries has assigned
two experienced and competent whaling inspectors to each of the two
whaling factory ships. SCAP directives require the Japanese to
obtain all of the biological and statistical data specified in Intor
national Whaling Conventions.
b. SCAP representatives are instructed to insure Japanes
compliance with terms of the International Whaling Conventions and
provisions of SCAP directives relating to whaling. They are further
instructed to prepare comprehensive reports on all phases of activi
ties of the Japanese whaling fleet with special reference to toch-
niques of killing, flensing, processing, obtaining biological data
and methods used in predicting the occurrence of whales. They will
make a complete photographic record.
2. Reference radio requests SCAP file on the Antarctic Projoet
in anticipation of questions which might be raised at the forthcoming
International Whaling Conference.
a. In addition to State Department file of Radio messages
regarding whaling, the following inclosures may be of assistance:
(1) Copy of the original application from the Imperial
Japanese Government through Central Liaison Office, CLO No. 2416 (EA)
dated 19 May 1946, with inclosure, requesting permission to engage in
whaling in the Antarctic. (Incl No 2)
It may be advisable to point out that the Japanes
requested permission for the use of three factory ships and 18 killer
boats and that SCAP approved the use of only two factory ships and 12
killer boats.
(2) A copy of the Staff Study (dated 3 Aug 46) prepared
by Natural Resources Section on the subject of Japanese participation
in Antarctic Whaling. (Incl No. 3)
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 99 === (confidence 0.99)
C/8 to DS fr NR, f11e No. NR 800.217 (14 Nov
Subject: "Reply to Washington Radio Regarding J
Antarctic Whaling Project."
(3) Copy of SCAP directive SCAPIN 1103, 6 Aug
subject: "Japanese Whaling Operations In The Antarctic". (Inol
No 4 and 5)
(4) Copies of SCAP directives relating to the
whaling industry. (Inc1 No 6, 7, 9, 11, 12, 13, 14, and 15)
(5) Copy of the instructions issued to SCAP repre-
sentatives accompanying the Antarctic Whaling expedition. (Incl
No 8)
b. In addition to SCAP directives inclosed hereyith,
a directive will be published on 15 Nov 46 informing the  apanese
Government that the flag to be flown by the Antarctic Whaling
vessels will be the Japanese Merchant Marine Flag, flown in the
usual manner plus the modified International code flag E. (Incl
No 19)
3. Part two (2) reference radio states, "both British and
New Zealand Governments have again raised questions of SCAP
directive 23 Aug 46 regarding conversion of three tankers to
whaling ship factories".
a. Conversion of two of the tankers referred to was
approved so the ships could be utilized in 1946-47 Antarctic
whaling. The third vessel was to be completed in May 1947 for
use in waters in the authorized fishing and whaling area adjacent
to Japan only. (Incl No 16 and 17) Company officials declared
the third vessel could be used for whaling operations in the
Bonin Islands since use of land stations by the Japanese in this
area is prohibited by SCAP directives. Company officials further
stated that the vessel could be used as a mother ship for tuna
boats and trawlers. It was planned that the vessel would be
used jointly by the three major Japanese whaling companies.
b. Recently an investigation of the practicability of
the use of this vessel in local waters was made by the Natural
Resources Section. The investigation disclosed that the vessel
would only be practical if used jointly by the three companies
because of the limited whale supply beyond reach of land sta-
tions in the presently authorized fishing and whaling area
around the Japanese home islands. Furthermore, the Japanese
Bureau of Fisheries will permit the use of a factory ship of
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIA
this size in loeal waters only on condition that it be
jointly. Recently the other two whaling companies decid
would not participate in its joint use. Consequently, the
of the vessels have decided to suspend conversion. The hull
will be completed only to a point where launching is possible
The hull will then be launched and moored but no further oon-
struction will be accomplished until such time as a decision hi
been reached regarding its future use.
Lt Col R.H. Fiedler, O-183587, Former Chief of the
Fisheries Division, Natural Resources Section, GHQ, SCAP, is now
in Washington, D.C. He is familiar with Japanese whaling probloms
It is believed he may be reached through the U.S. Fish and
Wildlife Service or U.S. Army Civil Affairs Division, Washington,
D.C.

JA
Washington
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's instrue-
tion No. 298, October 31, 1946, and previous correspondonce
regarding the reported taking of a right whale in the course
of whaling operations in the Bonin Islands during the 1945
season.
218
The Natural Resources Section, General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, reports that if a
TO:
right whale were caught in the Bonin Islands in 1945, it
would have been taken before the cessation of hostilities.
FE
The whaling season in the Bonin Islands area is normally
2ESP
December until April or May with the peak of the season in
March and April. General Headquarters authorized Japanese
Bonin Island whaling operations from December 1, I945 to
1JK
April 30, 1946. However, due to lack of equipment, opera-
IJA
tions did not commence until February 24, 1946. No right
whales were taken during this season. Therefore, if one
were taken in 1945 it would have been "two seasons" ago.
All biological and statistical data relative to this and other
operations required under the provisions of the International
Onahsonnan
Conventions for the regulation of whaling were forwarded by
Do.tello9g9
General Headquarters to the Adjutant General, Washington,
D. C., by 1etters of transmittal, AG 800.92 (25 May 1946) NR,
subject: "Compilation of Whaling Information," and AG 800.92
2h. Deasor
(25 June 1946) NR.

these operations.

Resources Section, General Headquarters, by the Jap
Bureau of Fisheries, no right whales were captured in
The records do show one whale listed as "others." An
investigation disclosed that this whale was a "bottle nose"
whale captured on October 14, 1945, twenty miles from
Akkeshi, Hokkaido.
Respectfully yours,
Georgé Atcheson, Jr.

Enolosure to Despatch No. 730 dated

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Natural Resources Section
APO 500
21 November 1946
NR 201.22( 21 November 1946 )Ad
SUBJECT: Letter of Appreciation
TO
Ambassador George Atcheson, Jr., Chief of
Diplomatic Section, General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, AP
500, U. S. Army
1. The Natural Resources Section wishes to expres
its sincere appreciation for the exceptional work per-
formed by Mr. Max W. Bishop of your section during the
final phases of the organization and administration of
Japanese whaling expedition prior to departure for the
Antarctic. Mr. Bishop was most cooperative and an in-
valuable source of advice, information, and assistance
during the trying period preceding the completion of the
project.
2. This Section, in order to correlate the work of
various sections of General Headquarters, Supreme Command
for the Allied Powers, involved in the whaling projeet,
was required to instigate action on many and varied subjo
and phases of this enormous task. This action was
initiated in the form of check sheets, memoranda, and
radio messages. Mr. Bishop handled certain phases of the
project efficiently and expeditiously, establishing him-
self as a competent executive and administrator, and
representative of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers.
3. The success of the organization and adminis-
tration of the whaling expedition is due in no small
part to the tireless efforts of Mr. Bishop, and the
Natural Resources Section is most appreciative of the
assistance received from him.
signed: Hubert G. Schenck
HUBERT G. SCHENCK
Lt Col
CB
Chief, Natural Resources Section

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.97)
UNITED

59t.6210
FROM : IR - Mr. Flory
sUBJECT: International Whaling Conference.
The Conference was convened on November 20, 1946 and the final
session was held on December 2, 1946. The scope of the Conferemes
was limited to conservation problems. The results can be defined
as (1) the codification and expansion of existing regulations and
(2) the establishment of an International Whaling Commission to amend
these regulations from time to time in the future as conditions mmy
require.
The matter of Japanese whaling did not arise at any session of
the Conference, and therefore in accordance with our conversation
at the time you cleared the instructions to the United States
Delegation, we felt you would not be interested in the day by day
progress of the Conference.
A
We enclose for your files two copies of Conference Paper Iwc/64
which contains the final documents of the Conference. Since
regulations have already been agreed to for the 1946/47 whaling
12-546
season, the Protocol contained in the enclosed papers (item III)
is applicable to the 1947/48 whaling season and the Convention (item
1I) is applicable to the 1948/49 and subsequent whaling seasons.
Under separate cover we are forwarding copies of Conference
Paper IwC/64 with covering instruction for your clearance prior
to tranamittal to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
1216146
NEST
IR:ECastleman:bmd
12/5/46

The Conference met at Washington on November 20, 1946,
under the Temporary Chairmanship of Remington Kellogg, Chair-
man of the Delegation of the United States of America.
At the opening session a motion was unanimously adopted
to extend an invitation for officers of the Secretariat of
the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations to
attend the Conference sessions and committee meetings as
observers. The attendance in this capacity of the officers
nominated by the Organization, D. B. Finn, Harry Winsor, and
H. V. Knight, was approved by the Conference, on the recommen-
dation of the Committee on Credentials.
With the approval of the President of the United States
of Americe, Clarke L. Willard, Acting Chief of the Division
of International Conferences, Dupartment of State of the
United States, was designated as Sccretary General of the
Conference, and Donald J. Chaney, Chief Counsel, Fish and
Wildlife Service, Department of the Interior of the United
States, was designated Technical Secretary of the Conference

=== Page 125 === (confidence 0.99)
Final Act
-7-
Remington Kellogg, Chairman of the Delegation of the
United States of America, was elected Permanent Chairman of
the Conference at the second session, held on November 20,
1946, and Ira N. Gabrielson, Member of the Delegation of the
United States of America, was elected Vice Chairman of the
Conference at the same session.
The general cimmittees established by the Rules of Pro-
cedure adonted provislonally at the opening session were con-
stituted by the Temporary Chairman as follows:
COMMITTEE ON CREDENTIALS
G. R. Fowles (New Zealand) - Chairman
A. R. Edwards (Chile)
P. F. Erichsen (Denmark)
Frances E. Pringle - Secretary
COMMITTEE ON NOMINATIONS
A. T. A. Dobson (United Kingdom) - Chairman
Olafur Bjornsson (Iceland)
Birger Bergorsen (Norway)
William L. Breese - Secretary
The following technical committees were appointed by the
Chairman:
COMMITTEE ON NETHERLANDS PROPOSAL
A. T. A. Dobson (United Kingdom) - Chairman

The final sessicn was held on December 2, 1946.
As a result of the deliberations of the Conference, the
following instruments were formulated and opened for signa-
ture on December 2, 1946, to remain open for signature for
fourteen days thereafter:
International Convention for the Regulation of Whaling
Protocol for the Regulation of Whaling
(hereinafter referred to as the Convention and the Protocol,
respectively).
The following resolutions and recommendations were
adopted:
工
The International Whaling Conference
RESOLVES:
1. To express its gratitude to the President of the
United States of America, Harry S. Truman, for his initiative
in convening the present Conference and for its preparation;
2. To express to its Chairman, Remington Kellogg, its
deep appreciation for the admirable manner in which he has
guided the Conference;

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.99)
Final Act
-11-
3. To express to the officers and staff of the Secre-
tariat its appreciation for their untiring services and dilf
gent efforts in contributing to the attainment of the objec-
tives of the Conference.
II
The International Whaling Conference
RESOLVES:
That the Government of the United States of America be
authorized to publish the Final Act of this Conference, the
text of the Convention and of the Protocol, and to make avail-
able for publication such additioual documents in connection
with the work of this Conference as in its judgment may be
considered in the public interest.
III
The International Whaling Conference
RESOLVES:
That all signatury governments should draw the attention
of their inspectors and of the whaling companies operating
under their jurisdiction to previous cases of taking baleen
whales in the closed season on the pretext of providing
fenders for the bunkering of whale catchers. The Conference
desires to emphasize that this practice constitutes an
infringement of paragraph 7 of the Schedule annexed to the
Convention and reconmends that if it is desired to send whale
catchers long distances in the open sea before the commence-
ment of or after the end of the whaling season, suitable
arrangements must be made for bunkering them without the use
of carcasses of baleen whales.

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.99)
Final Act
-12-
IV
The International Whaling Conference
RECOMMENDS:
That the chart of Nomenclature of Whales annexed to this
Final Act be accepted as a guide by the governments repre-
sented at the Conference.
V
The Intornational Whaling Conference
RECOMMENDS:
That the International Whaling Commission provided for
in Article III of the Convention (hereinafter referred to as
the Commission) should review the prohibition on the use of
factory ships, or whale catchers attached thereto, for the
purpose of taking humpback whales in any waters south of 400
South Latitude after taking into consideration the biological
and other data available and consider the desirability of
either the removal of the prohibition after the 1948-49 sea-
son and the southern winter season of 1949 or alternatively
a limitation of the number of humpback whales to be taken
both in the Antarctic and tropical areas.
VI
The International Whaling Conference
RECOMMENDS:
That the Comnission should keep under constant review
the question of the limits of the Antarctic whaling season
and also the maximum number of blue-whale units as defined by
paragraph 8 (b) of the Schedule permitted to be taken during
the season.

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
Final Act
- 13 -
VII
The Internatfonel Whaling Conference
CONSIDERS:
That the conditions relating to the use of factory ships
within territorial waters of Contracting Governments as pro-
vided by paragraph 17 of the Schedule should be kept under
revicw by the Commission so as to insure that these operations
are conducted on an economic basis.
VIII
The Intornational Whaling Conference
RECOMMENDS:
That when adequate information becomes available concern-
ing the migration routes and seasons in localities where land
stations are maintained and operated, specific annual open
periods for whaling should be prescribed instead of the regu-
lation included in the Schedule as paragraph 10. It is the
viow of the Conference that the Commission should endeavor to
obtain at the earliest possible time scientific information as
a basis for prescribing specific seasons during which land
stations shall be permitted to operete in the various areas.
IX
At the request of the Delegation of the Union of Soviet
Socialist Republics, the following statements are included:
1. Owing to unforeseen circumstances the Soviet fac-
tory ship will be unable to reach the wheling grounds by the
commencement of the 1945-47 season in waters south of 400
South Latitude. Accordingly the Delegation of the Union of
Soviet Socialist Republics requests that this ship be per-
mitted to operate with a full complement of catchers for a

=== Page 132 === (confidence 0.99)
Rinal Act
-14-
continuous period of four months from the date on which it
1s able to commence operations in that area. Note 1s.made
that a similar concession was made to other governments for
the season 1945-46.
2. The Delegation of the Union of Soviet Socialist
Republics also requests that the factory ship be permitted
to conduct operations in that area for the four-month period
during the season 1946-47 without regard to the sixteen
thousand blue-whale unit catch limitation.
The Conference supports these requests and considers
them justified in the special circumstances. It is under-
stood th?t support by the Conference of these requests shall
not be rogarded as a precedent for future seasons.
X
The International Whaling Conference
SUPPCRTS
and considers justified the request of the Delegation
of the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics that the taking
of gray whales in the Bering and Chukotsk seas should be
pormitted when the meat and products of such whales aro to
be used exclusively for local consumption by the aborigines
of the Chukotsk and Korjaksk areas.
XI
The International Whaling Conference
RECOGNIZES:
The desirability of achieving a large measure of uni-
formity among the various Contracting Governments with respect
to thc nature and severity of penalties imposed, for contra-
ventions of the Convention, upon persons or ships operating

most favorable management and with the momt

view of the Conference that it is desirable that the Gov
ments should provide for the imposition of penalties su

sion should study the reports regarding infractions made
them in accordance with the provisions of Article Ix, 4.
the Convention, with a view to making recommendations to

for the purpose of achieving the greatest possible uniformi
in the penalties imposed for contraventions of the Conventi

A D D E N D U M
As  result of the discussion of certain matt
at the Conference, the Netherlands Delegate invited
ference to include in its Final Act a resolution in
lowing terms:
"The Conference recommends that in the in
cf effective conservation and cevelopment or wn
stocks the Governments represented at the Confe
refrain from taking any measures which might prey
any country adhering to the principles of the int
national whaling agreements from ratifying or on
ing into the international regulations for therp
servation of whale stocks."
This resolution was, however, defeated by nine vo
to three, some delegates disagreeing with the subatono
the resolution and others considering that it contatr
implications outside the purview of the Conference
express request of the Netherlands Delegate these
recorded in this addendum to the Final Aot.

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.99)
RENCE
- 21 -
INTERNATIONAL WHALING CONFERENCE· WASHINGTON,D.C.
INTERNATIONAL CONVENTION FOR THE REGULATION OF WHALING
The Governments whose duly authorized representatives have
subscribed hereto,
Recognizing the interest of the nations of the world in
safeguarding for future generations the great natural resources
represented by the whale stocks;
Considering that the history of whaling has seen over-
fishing of one area after another and of one species of whale
after another to such a degree that it is essential to protect
all species of whales from further overfishing;
Recognizing that the whale stocks are susceptible of
natural increases if whaling is properly regulated, and that
increases in the size of whale stocks will permit increases in
the numbers of whales which may be captured without endanger-
ing these natural resources;
Recognizing that it is in the common interest to achieve
the optimum level of whele stocks as rapidly as possible with-
out causing wide-spread economic and nutritional distrese;
Recognizing that in the course of achieving these objec-
tives, whaling operations should be confined to those species
best able to sustain exploitation in order to give an interval
for recovery to certain species of whales now depleted in num-
bers;
Desiring to establish a system of international regulation

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.99)
Convention
- 22 -
for the whale fisheries to ensure proper and effectiv
servation and development of whale stocks on the basis or
principles embodied in the provisions of the Internationel
Agreement for the Regulation of Whaling signed in London on
June 8, 1937 and the protocols to that Agreement signed in
London on June 24, 1938 and November 26, 1945; and
Having decided to conclude a convention to provide for
the proper conservaticn of whale stocks and thus make possi-
ble the orderly development of the whaling industry;
Have agreed as follows:
Article I
1. This Convention includes the Schedule attached
thereto which forms an integral part thereof. All references
to "Convention" slhall be understood as including the said
Schedule either in its present terms or as amended in accord-
ance with the provisions of Articlc V.
2. This Convention applies to factory ships, land sta-
tions, and whale catchers under the jurisdiction of the Con-
tracting Governments, and to all waters in which whaling is
prosecuted by such factory ships, land stations, and whale
catchers.
Article II
As used in this Convention
1. "factory ship" means a ship in which or on which
whales are treated whether wholly or in part;
2. "land station" means a factory on the land at which
whales are treated whether wholly or in part;
3. "whale catcher" means a ship used for the purpose of

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
Convention
一
- 23 -
hunting, taking, towing, holding on to, or scouting for
whales;
4. "Contracting Government" means any Government which
has deposited an instrument of ratification or has given notice
of adherence to this Convention.
Article III
1. The Contracting Governments agree to establish an
International Whaling Commission, hereinafter referred to as
the Commission, to be composed of one member from each Con-
traciing Government. Each member shall have one vote and
may be accompanied by one or more experts and advisers.
2. The Commission shall elect from its own members a
Chairman and Vice Chairman and shall determine its own Rules
of Procedure. Decisions of the Commission slall be taken by
a simple majority of those nembers voting except that a three-
fourths majority of those members voting shall be reguired
for action in pursuance of Article V. The Rules of Procedure
may provide for decisions otherwise than at meetings of the
Commission.
3. The Commission may arpoint its own Secretary and
staff.
4. The Commission may set up, from among its own mem-
bers and experts or advisers, such committees as it considers
desirable to perform such functions as it may authorize.
5. The expenses of each member of the Commission and
of his experts and advisers shall be determinei and paid by
his own Government.
6. Recognizing that specialized agencies related to
the United Nations will be concerned with the conservation

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.99)
Convontion
-24-
and development of whale fisheries and the products ari
therefrom and desiring to avoid duplication of function, th
Contracting Governments will consult among themselves within
two years after the coming into force of this Convention to
decide whether the Commission shall be brought within the
framework of a specialized agency related to the United Nations.
7. In the meantime the Government of the United Kingdom
of Great Britain and Northern Ireland shall arrange, in con-
sultation with the other Contracting Governments, to convene
the first meeting of the Commission, and shall initiate the
consultation referred to in paragraph 6 above.
8. Subsequent meetings of the Commission shall be
convened as the Commission may determine.
Article IV
1. The Commission may cither in collaboration with or
through independent agencies of the Contracting Governments
or other public or private agencies, establishments, or
organizations, or independently
(a) encourage, recommend, or if necessary, organ-
ize studies and investigations relating to
whales and wheling;
(b) collect and analyze statistical information
concerning the current condition and trend of
the whale stocks and the effects of whaling
activities thereon;
(c) study, appraise, and disseminate information
concerning methods of maintaining and
increasing the populations of whale-stocks.

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
Convention
-25-
2. The Commission shall arrange for the publication
reports of its activities, and it may publish independently
or in collaboration with the International Bureau for Whaling
Statistics at Sandefjord in Norway and other organizations
and agencies such reports as it deems appropriate, as well as
statistical, scientific, and other pertinent information relat-
ing to whales and whaling.
Article V
1. The Commission may amend from time to time the pro-
visions of the Schedule by adopting regulations with respect
to the conservation and utilization of whele resources, fixing
(a) protected and unprotected species; (b) open and closed
seasons; (c) open and closed waters, including the designation
of sanctuary areas; (d) size limits for each species; (e) time,
methods, and intensity of whaling (including the maximum catch
of whales to be taken in any one season); (f) types and speci-
fications of gear and apparatus and appliances which may be
used; (g) methods of measurement; and (h) catch returns and
other statistical and biological records.
2. These amendments of the Schedule (a) shall be such
as are necessary to carry out the objectives and purposes of.
this Convention and to provide for the conservation, develop-
ment, and optimum utilization of the whale resources; (b)
shall be based on scientific findings; (c) shall not involve
restrictions on the number or nationality of factory ships or
land stations, nor allocate specific quotas to any factory
ship or land station or to any group of factory ships or land
stations; and (d) shall take into consideration the interests

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
26
of the consumers of whale products and the whaling
3. Each of such amendments shall become efreoti
respect to the Contracting Governments ninety days foll
notification of the amendment by the Commission to each
the Contracting Governments, except that (a) if any Gov
presents to the Commission objection to any amendment prio
the expiration of this ninety-day period, the amendment she
not become effective with respect to any of the Government
for an additional ninety days; (b) thereupon, any other Con
tracting Government may present objection to the amendment
any time prior to the expiration of the additional ninety-d
period, or before the expiration of thirty days from the dat
of receipt of the last objection recei ed during such addi-
tional ninety-day period, whichever date shall be the later
and (c) thereafter, the amendment shall become effective with
respect to all Contracting Governments which have not present
objection but shall not become effective with respect to any
Government which has so objected until such date as the objed
tion is withdrawn. The Commission shall notify each Contract
ing Government imnediately upon receipt of each objection and
withdrawal and each Contracting Government shall acknowledge
receipt of all notifications of amendments, objections, and
withdrawals.
4. No amendments shall become effective before July
1549.
Article VI
The Commission may from time to t me make
to any or all Contracting Governments on any

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.99)
Convention
-.27-
relate to whales or whaling and to the objectives and purpom
of this Convention.
Article VII
The.Contracting Governments shall ensure prompt trans-
mission to the International Bureau for Whaling Statistics
at Sandefjord in Norway, or to such other body as the Commis-
sion may designate, of notifications and statistical and
other information required by this Convention in such form
and manner as may be prescribed by the Commission.
Articlo VIII
1. Notwithstanding anything contained in this Convention,
any Contracting Government may grant to any of its nationals a
special permit authorizing that national to kill, take, and
treat whales for purposes of scientific research subject to
such restrictions as to number and subject to such other con-
ditions as the Contracting Government thinks fit, and the kill-
ing, taking, and treating of whales in accordance with the
provisions of this Article shall be exempt from the operation
of this Convention. Each Contracting Government shall report
at once to the Commission all such authorizations which it
has granted. Each Contracting Government may at any. time re-
voke eny such special permit which it has granted.
2. Any whales teken under these special permits shall
so far as practicable be processed and the proceeds shall be
dealt with in accordance with directions issued by the Gov-
ernment by which the permit was granted.
3. Each Contracting Government shall transmit to such
body as may be designated by tre Commission, insofar as

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
Convention
- 28 -
practicable, and at intervals of not more than one year,
scientific information available to that Government with
respect to whales and whaling, including the results of re-
search conducted pursuant to paragraph 1 of this Article and
to Article IV.
4. Recognizing that continuous collection and analysis
of biological data in connection with the operations of fac-
tory ships and land stations are indispensable to sound and
constructive management of the whele fisheries, the Contract-
ing Governments will take all practicable measures to obtain
such data.
Article IX
1. Each Contracting Government shall take appropriate
measures to ensure the application of the provisions of this
Convention and the punishment of inrractions against the said
provisions in operations carried out by persons or by vassels
under its jurisdicticn.
2. No bonus or other remuneration calculated with re-
lation to the results of their work shall be paid to the
gunners and crews of whale catchers in respect of any whales
the taking of vhich is forbidden by this Convention.
3. Prosecution for infractions against or contraventions
of this Convention shall be instituted by the Government hav-
ing jurisdiction over the ofiense.
4. Each Contracting Government shall transmit to the
Commission full details of each infraction of the provisions
of this Convention by persons or vessels under the jurisdic-
tion of that Govarnment as reported by its inspectors. This
information shall include a statement of measures taken for

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.99)
Convention
- 29 -
dealing with the infraction and of penalties imposed.
Article X
1. This Convention shall be ratified and the instruments
of ratification shall be deposited with the Government of the
United States of America.
2. Any Government which has not signed this Convention.
may adhere thereto after it enters into force by a notification
in writing to the Government of the United States of America.
3. The Government of the United States of America shall
inform all other signatory Governments and all adhering Gov-
ernments of all ratifications deposited and adherences re-
ceived.
4. This Convention shall, when instruments of ratifica-
tion have been deposited by at least six signetory Governments,
which shall include the Gcvernments of the Netherlands,
Norway, the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics, the United
Kingdon of Great Britain and Northern Ireland, and the United
States of America, enter into force with respect to those Gov-
ernments and shall enter intô force'with respect to each Gov-
ernment which subsequently ratifies or adheres on the date of
the deposit of its instrument of ratification or tha receipt
of its notification of adherence.
5. The provisions of the Schedule shall not apply prior
to July 1, 1948. Amendments to the Schedula adopted pursuant
to Article V shall not apply prior to July 1, 1949.
Article XI
Any Contracting Government may withdraw from this Conven-
tion on June thirty of any year by giving notice on or before

=== Page 151 === (confidence 0.99)
Convention
-30-
January first of the same year to the depositary Government,
which upon receipt of such a notice shall at once communicate
it to the other Contracting Governments. Any other Contract-
ing Government may, in like manner, within one month of the
receipt of a copy of such a notice from the depositary Gov-
ernment, give notice of withdrawal, so that the Convention
shall cease to be in force on June thirtieth of the sama year
with respect to the Gevernment giving such notice of with-
drawal.
This Convention shall bear the date on which it is opened
for signature and shall remain open for signature for a period
of fourteen days thereafter.

SCHEDULE
1. (a) There sha"l be maintained on each factory ship
at least two inspectors of whaling for the
purpose of maintaining twenty-four hour inspec-
tion. These inspectors shal? be appointed and
paid by the Government having jurisdiction over
the factory ship.
(b)
Adequate inspection shall be maintained at each
land station. The inspectors serving at each
land station shall be appointed and paid by the
Government having jurisdiction over the land
station.
2. It is forbidden to take or kill gray whales or right
whales, except when the meat and products of such whales are
to be used exclusively for local consumption by the aborigi-
nes.
3. It is forbidden to take or kill calves or suckling
whales or female whales which are accompanied by calves or
suckling whales.
4. It is forbidden tc use a factory ship or a whale
catcher attached thereto for the purpose of taking or treat-
ing baleen whales in any of the following area:

cept that from 1500 East Longitude eastward

catcher shall be permitted between 66o North
Latitude and 72o North Letitude;
(b)
in the Atlantic Ocean and its dependent waters
north of 40º South Latitude;
(c)
in the Pacific Ocean and its dependent waters
east of 1500 West Longitude between 400 South
Latitude and 35° North Latitude;
(a)
in the Pacific Ocean and its dependent waters
west of 1500 West Longitude between 400 South
Latitude and 200 North Latitude;
(e)
in the Ir lian Ocean and its dependent waters
north of 40o South Latitude.

catcher attached thereto for the purpose of taking or treat-

tude.

catcher attached thereto for the purpose of taking or treat-
ing humpback whales in any waters south of 400 South Lati-
tude.

and sei whales of not less than 35 feet (10.7' meters) in
length may be taken for delivery to land stations provided
that the meat of such whales is to be used for local con-.
sumption as human or animal food.
Whales must be measured when at rest on deck or plat-
form, as accurately as possible by means of a steel tape
measure fitted at the zerc end with a spiked handle which can
be stuck into the deck planking abreast of one end of the
whale. The tape measure shall be stretched in a straight
line parallel with the whale's body and read abreast the
other end of the whale. The ends of the whale, for measure-
ment purposes, shall be the pcint of the upper jaw and the
notch between the tail flukes. Measurements, after being
accurately read cn the tape measure, shall be logged to the
nearest foot: that is to say, any whale between 75'6" and
76'6" shal1 be logged as 76', and any whale between 76'6"
and 77'6" shall be logged as 77'. The measurement of any
whale which falls on an exact half foot shall be logged at
the next half foot, e.&. 76'6" precisely, shall be logged
as 77'.
10. It is forbidden to use a land station or a whale
catcher attached thereto for the purpose of taking or
treating baleen whales in any area or in any waters for
more than six months in any period of twelve months, such
period of six months to be contintous.
11. It is forbidden to use a factory ship which has
been used during a season in any waters scuth of 4o0 South

12. (a) All whales taken shall be delivered to the
factory ship or land station and all parts
of such whales ahall be processed by boiling
or otherwise, except the internal organs,
whale bone and flippers of all whales, the
meat of sperm whales and of parts of whales
intended for human food or feeding animals.
(b)
Complete treatment of the c&rcasses of.
"Dauhval" and of whales used as fenders will

13. The taking of whales for delivery to a factory
ship shall be so regulated or restricted by the master or
person in charge of the factory ship that no whale carcass
(except of a whale used as a fender) shall remain in the
sea for a longer period than thirty-three hours from the
time of killing to the time when it is taker. up on to the
deck of the factory ship for treatment. All whale catch-
ers engaged in taking whales must report by radio to the
factory ship the time when each whale is caught.
14. Gunners and crews of factory ships, land stations,
and whale catchers, shall be engaged on such terms that
their remuneration shall depend to a considerable extent
upon such factors as the species, size, and yield of
whales taken and not merely upon the number of the whales
taken. No bonus or other remuneration shall be paid to

the gunners or crews of whale catchers in respect of the

15. Copies of all official laws and regulations relat-
ing to whales and whaling and changes in such laws and reg-
ulations shall be transmitted to the Commission.
16. Notification shall be given in accordance with the
provisions of Article VII of the Convention with regard to
all factory ships and land stations of statistical informa-
tion (a) concerning the number of whales of each species
taken, the number thereof lost, and the number treated at
each factory ship or land station, and (b) as to the aggre-
gate amounts of oil of each grade and quantities of meal,
fertilizer, (guano), and other products derived from them,
together with (c) particulars with respect to each whale
treated in the factory ship or land station as to the date
and approximate latitude and longitude of taking, the spe-
cies and sex of the whale, its length and, if it contains a
foetus, the length and sex, if ascertainable, of the foetus.
The data referred to in (a) and (c) above shall be verified
at the time of the tally and there shall also be notifica-
tion to the Commission of any information which may be col-
lected or obtained concerning the calving grounds and migra-
tion routes of whales.
In communicating this information there shall be
specified:
(a)
the name and gross tonnage of each factory ship;
(b)
the number and aggregate gross tonnage of the
whale catchers;

17. Notwithstanding the definition of land station con-
tained in Article II of the Convention, a factory ship oper-

the movements of which are confined solely to the territorial
watcrs of that Government shall be subject to the regulations
governing the operation of land stations within the following

(a) on the coast of Madagascar and its dependencies,

(b) on the west coast of Australia in the area
known as Shark Bay and northward to Northwest
Cape and including Exmouth Gulf and King
George's Sound, including the port of Albany;
and on the east coast of Australia, in Twofold
Bay and Jervis Bay.

INTERNATIONAL WHALING CONFERENCE · WASHINGTON,D.C.
PROTOCOL FOR THE REGULATION OF WHALING
The Governments whose duly authorized representatives
have subscribed hereto,
Recognizing the necessity of an early decision regarding
the regulations to be made applicable to the whaling season
of 1947-48;
Having due regard both to the world shortage of oil and
fats and to the necessity for the conservation of the whale
stocks;
Agree as follows:
Article I
All the provisions of the Protocol for the Regulation of
Whaling signed in London on November 26, 1945 shall be made
applicable as if in the said Protocol the words "season
1947-48" were substituted for the words "season 1946/47" and
the words "1 May 1948 to 31 October 1948" were substituted
for the words "1st May, 1947, to 31st October, 1947".
Article II
This Protocol shall come into force when notifications of
acceptance thereof shall have been given to the Covernment of
the United States of America by all the Governments parties to
the Protoco1 of November 26, 1945.

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
Protocol
IWC/64
-42-
This Protocol shall bear the date on which it is opened
for signature and shall remain open for signature for a period
of fourteen days thereafter.
IN WITNESS WHEREOF the undersigned, being duly authorized,
have signed this Protocol.
DONE in Washington this second day of December, 1946, in
the English language, the original of which sh ll be deposited
in the archives of the Government of the United States of
America. The Government of the United States of America shall
transmit certified copies thereof to all the other signatory
and adhering Governments.

Dear Mr. Borton,
On reviewing the correspondence on the Japanese
Antarctic Whaling Expedition, and the Supreme Commander's
directives on the subject, I find that there are three
points that are perhaps not completely covered, although
in practice they may have been satisfactorily dealt with.
I mentioned them to Mr. Allison on the lOth October and
he said that they had already been included in a list of
questions sent to the Supreme Commander.
Point No. 1 is in regard to the observance of modern
whaling practice. (Please see paragraph 4 (f)
of our Aide Memoire of 3rd September).
Point No. 2 concerns the definition of authority of
the inspectors. SCAPIN No. 1321 of 6th November
deals with the appointment of two United States
CS/V
Army Officers but it does not refer. to the
authority of inspectors generally. Perhaps there
has been an administrative order on the subject.
Point No. 3 relates to the reporting of catches to the
International Bureau of Whaling Statistics at
Sandefjord, Norway. In SCAPIN No. 1323 of 7th
November radio communications are provided for
but no reference is made to the reporting of
catches to Sandefjord. This is, as you know,
8
obligatory under the Conventions, so perhaps the
6
arrangement is that the reports will be relayed
7
from Tokyo. It would however be convenient if
we could be assured that Sandefjord will be proper-
ly informed.
2.
You also mentioned to me that work had been. suspended
on the Tanker referred to in the Supreme Commander''s direct-
ive of 23rd August, 1946. Would you be good enough to let me
know the phraseology in which the Japanese Government is
ordered to discontinue the work?
N-91947
I
Yours sincerely,
9
Mr. Hugh Borton,

Memorandum of Conversations
DATE: December 17
894.628/1
and 18, 1946
SUBJECT:
Flag of Antarctie Whaling Expedition.
PARTICIPANTS: r. Graves, ounselor, Br1tish Embasey
Mr. Emmerson, Division of Jep nese Affairs
2
1
5
4347
Kr. raves of the Eritish Embessy telephoned to me on
December 17th to sey that he had just noticed a SCAP directive
to the Japenese Government epecifying that the Antarctic Whaling
Expelition woul, fly the Japanese Merchant Marine flag. He
stated that this was contrary to our previous assurances and re-
cuested clorification of the matter before he reported it to
London.
1 referreu to our note to the ritiuh Emt sty of November Plst
which stated that the whaling expedition would use the SCAJAP
flag 'E'" which consisted of the Japanese Merchant Marine flag
CS/V
plus the modified international "E". I called Mr. Graves back
on December 18th and stated to him as followe:
"SCAJAP flag 'E'" denote the Japanese Merchant Marine
flag plue the modified international "E". The Japanese
Merchant Marine flag flown with modified international "E"
at the peak has been accepted for over a year in Hongkong,
ILEL
Singapore, Melbourne, Sydney, Brisbane, and in Dutoh and
Chinese ports in the Pacific, and is therefore well known
and accepted in Pacific waters."

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.99)
NATURAL RESOURCES SECTION
PEPORT NUMBER 63
23 December 1946
GLOSSARY OF JAPANESE FISHERIES TERMS 1/
1. Fish and fishing play such an important role in Japanese life
that an extensive and complicated fisheries vocabulary has evolved. Each
of the hundreds of kinds of fish, shellfish, and seaweed has several
vernacular names; the wide assortment of prepared seafood adds many wore
words; and the variety of fishing gear has a large specialised nomen-
clature.
2. An interpreter or translator who is not trained in fisheries
terminology finds himself confused by the many Japanese terms, some of
which have no exact counterpart in English. The Japanese when translating
their own phraseology into English make frequent unintentional mistakes
because of the complicated nature of the subject matter. Many organisms
which are abundant in Japan are not to be found in any part of the
English-speaking world, and attempts at translation are often inaccurate.
3. This glossary was prepared to establish a standardized vocabulary
It should help the members of the Occupation Forces to understand Japanese
publications and reports. It should help the Japanese authorities in
'translating their reports into English.
4. The best accepted common names for commercially important marine
and freshwater animals and plants are presented. Countless local vernacu-
lar names for fish, shellfish, and seaweed are in use in various parts of
Japan. Some of the most widely accepted of these names are presented, but
lack of space has precluded the inclusion of all local names. For example
one species of seaweed has at least ten vernacular names, only the most
commonly used of which are included.
5. Wherever possible those English words wnich have been incorrectly
used in Japanese translations are incl uded with appropriats notations.
This should help to clarify ambiguous statistical tables. The word
"Suzuki" can be taken as an example of mis-translation. "Suzuki" is the
name for the common sea-bass and is sometimes translated as such. How-
ever, in many reports it is translated as perch or sea-perch for no
apparent reason. "Kamasu", wiich is the word for barracuda, is translated
as saury-pike in a standard dictionary and is sometimes rendered as pike,
two fishes which have only a superficial resemblance to the barracuda.
To complicate the matter further, young barracuda, "kokamasu", is some-
1/ This report was prepared by Captain Richard S. Croker, Fisheries
Division.
3

=== Page 208 === (confidence 0.99)
times translated as pickerel. It can readily be seen how the unwary are
led astray by loose translations.
6. General terms wnich refer to groups of plants or animals occur
in the Japanese language as in others. However, a general name often
applies not only to a group of species but to a particular species as
well. Thus "fuedai" is the name applied to snappers of the family
Lutjanidae, yet the same word is the specific name of one species.
Lutjanus rivulatus. The prefix "ma" denotes a genuine or true species.
Thus "iwashi", which is the general term for small sardine-like fishes
and often applies to the common sardine, is usually rendered as "maiwashi"
when referring to the common sardine.
7. This report 1s based on the "Glossary for Japanese Fisheries"
prepared in 1945 by the Fisheries Section, Economic Branch, S-5 at the
Civil Affairs Holding and Staging Area, Presidio of Monterey, California.
and on a glossary prepared by the Japanese Bureau of Fisheries for the
use of its staff.
8. Practically all the species of crustaceans, molluscs and sea-
weeds which are used commercially and most of the food and gane fishes
and other aquatic vertebrates are included. The most widely used kinds
of prepared sea food are listed. Only the most commonly employed types
of fishing gear are presented.
9. The Japanese and scientific nomenclature of the fishes follows
"Keyes to the Fishes and Fish-like Animals of Japan" by Yaichiro Okada
and Kiyomatsu Matsubara, Sanseido Co Ltd, Tokyo, 1938. This 1s the
standard work on Japanese fishes. Even where this publication differe
from accepted American scientific terminology it has been followed in
order to facilitate references. Names of the less common species of
fishes which were omitted from this report because of space limitations
can be found in the above mentioned reference book. The nomenclature of
the other vertebrates is according to "A Catalogue of Vertebrates of
Japan" by Yaichiro Okada, Maruzen Co Ltd, Tokyo, 1938
10. A list prepared specially for this report by Professor Hiroshi
Niino of the Tokyo College of Fisheries furnished the nomenclature for
the edible molluscs and crustaceans. Professor Saburo Fujimori of the
Imperial Fisharies Experimental Station provided the nomenclature for the
seaweeds and t e oyster drills. Professor Yoshio Hiyama of the Tokyo
Imperial University and Professor Masamitsu Oshima formerly of the Tokyo
Prefectural Higher School, both of whom are on the staff of the Natural
Resources Section, gave invaluable assistance in the preparation of the
glossary, especially in providing common and scientific names of fishes
and in advising which species should be included.
11. Lists of names furnished by officials of the Tokyo Central Market
and the Japan Marine Products Co Ltd, and by other individuals, have been
integrated into the glossary.
4

=== Page 209 === (confidence 0.97)
12. Japanese words have been transcribed according to the Modified
Hepburn (Romaji) system of transliteration. In alphabetising the Japamese
words a strict letter sequence has been followed without regard to dia-
critical marks, hyphens or word separations.
5

E
FP
called kr. Allen to get his personal off-hand reaction to the
R
proposed United States counter-proposal to the Australian proposal to
the Subcommittee on Japanese fishing - specifically with respect to
the appropriateness of a 50-mile protective boundary around the United
States and United States' possessions.
ir. Allen said that there was a halibut ground southeast by east
of Kodiak Island which was about l00 miles from shore. Mr. Allen did
not know whether that area would be productive enough to support factory
operation on a sustained basis. However, ne knew that individual
vessels nad gone out from shore and had taken substantial catches from
the area. Mr. Allen rather aoubted that it would be feasible for Japan
to send a vessel over in order to exploit this one area.
894.628/
lir. Allen also inaicated that while there was a possibility of
taking salmon beyond 50 miles, he doubted whether it would be profitable
to send a ship from Japan to engage in operations outside of the 50
mile belt.
DES7
Mr. Allen

1/22/47

Following comments concerning proposed FEC 035/1 Jap
fishing, whaling and pearl fishing are offered with
recommendation that US strongly resist efforts of
8/1-1847
Australian representative to have this paper adopted.
Present SCAP policy re fisheries 1s governed by PEC O351
and SwnnC 99/3. It does not appear that any change to
this policy under prevailing conditions in Japan is
warranted. Present administration of Jap fisheries
reaults in interim actions only and no precedent for
permanent rights is thereby established or ccntemplated.
Existing directives cover every contingency which might
effect security of marine industries of Allied countries.
These industries are adequately and reasonably protected.
Proposed appropriations by FEc of administrative responsitility
in fishing and wheling appears to be unwarranted reflection
upon past and present administration by SCAP whose decisions
have not prejudiced Allied security or economy in slightest
measure. Proposed changes ignore humanitarian consider-
ations by oractically insuring reduced marine production
when world overall food shortage promises to remain acute.
s has a vital stake in augmenting this food supply as
demonstrated by burden which Jap shortages of food has
placed upon US alon. Alsc mandatory reference.to FEC will
causing lengthy and costly delays when emergency action
FILED
is required.
2
c

11
CONFIDENTIAL
5

=== Page 273 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-#13 January 18, from Tokyo
Detailed comment on objectionable paragraphs in pro-
posed FEC 035/1 follows:
(underline) 2(A) (end underline) Fishing and whaling operetion
subject to security consideretions, are being carried out
in defined area at present.  ective supervision of Jap
fishing and whaling industries requires timely decislons to
take advantage of fluctuations in fish and whale occurrences
and insure efficient use distribution available fishing
equipnent.
(underline) ?(B) (end underline) Would make it mandatory
for SCAP to request an amendment if for any reason a change
were desired. Amendment might require months of negotiation
and needlessly complicate administrative process in Japan.
Authorization of fishing in.present area under existing
directives 1s specifically stated in SCAPIN 1033 not to
be an expression of Allied policy relative to ultimate
determination of national jurisdiction or fishing rights
should be peace treaty matter and that FEC decision
present time would in fact again merely amount to an interim
measure.
(underline) 3 (A) and 3 (B) (end underline) Same comments
as above.
(underline) 3(C) (end underline) Fould preclude exploitation
whale occurrences in Bonin Isiand area. These islands are
too distant fron Japan to permit use of Jap shore stations.
Experts here estimate that approximately 300 wales may be
taken annually in Bonin Island area by operating factory ships,
a catch which can ill be spared at time of critical world
shortages. In addition bumanitarian principles of augmenting
supply of protein foods in Japan (as well as world supply
of fats and oils) cannot be over-looked. It is feit here
that Australian propose, undoubtedly in part arises out of
present SCAP-administerod and Jap-manned Antarctic whaling
project. As the Department is aware, however, this whaling
fleet was dispatched as an emergency measure, was authorized
for one season only, and no percedent for future authorization
was intended or implted.
(underline) 4 (end uuderline) No comment.
In summátion, it is felt
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
-3- # 13 January 18, from Tokyo
In summation, it is felt that adoption by FEC of policy
contemplated in FEC 035/1 would be tantamount to placing undue
restriction upon administrative processes which are inherent.
in SCAP authority. Existing directives already amply
protect interests of all Allied Nations, including Australia,
and proposed chenges would appear to result in "tinkering"
with the administration of present satisfactory policy,
thereby adding unnecessary confusion and restriction which
precedent indicates are unuecessary and unwarranted.
ATCHESON

SUMMARY
The whaling industry of Japan operates in two general areas: im
Japanese and coloniall waters and in Antarctio waters.
The number of whales caught in Japanese and colonial waters inereased
from 1,717 to 2,298, or about 30 percent between 1930 and 1939. In terms of
value, the increase in the whale catoh was even greater: from l.8 million
yen in 1930 to 6.1 million un 1939. The number of whales caught in colonial
waters decreased during this period, while the number caught in Japanese
waters increased. In the carly l930's the value of whales caught in colonial
waters was about one-half of the value of those caught in Japanese waters;
in 1938 and 1939 the value of the colonial catoh had declined to about one-
fifth of the value of whale catch in Japanese waters.
If the Japanese whaling industry continues to operate in Japanese
and colonial waters without great change, an annual catoh of about 2,000
whales may reasonably be expected. If some or all of the whale catoh in
colonial waters is lost to Japan in the future, the net loss to the industry
will be substantial.
Japanese Antarctic whaling began in late l934 and developed rapidly.
During 1934, one whaler plus auxiliary ships caught 213 whales; in 1938,
six whalers plus auxiliary ships caught 7,540. In terms of the total value
of manufactured products, the increase was notable: from 487 thousnd yen
I. Whaling in colonial waters refers to all whaling carried out by boats
that started from and returned to a port in Korea or Formosa.

about 90 percent or more of this value. Output of whale oil in the
jumped from 2,000 metric tons in 1934 to 81,000 metric tons in 1988. In 1980
the quantity of whale oil produced in Antarctic waters (26,000 metrio tons)
was equivalent to about 8 percent of the total supply of fats and oils predueed
in Japan in the same year.
Antarctic whaling in the future offers the possibility of providing either
oil for consumption in Japan or foreign exohange through whale oil sales abroad.
The Japanese share of future Antarotic whaling will depend upon Allied poliey.
Unless severe restrictions are imposed, Antarotic whaling may be of considerable
economic significance to the Japanese in the future.

I. WHALING IN JAPANESE AND COLONIAL WATERS
AoDevelopment
The over-all mumber and value of whales caught in Japanese and
colonial waters inoreased during the period 1930-39. The mumber of whales
caught inoreased from 1,717 to 2,298, or 30 peroent; the total valwe of thé
catoh trebled, rising from 1,859,000 to 6,115,000 yen (Table 1). In 1981
and 1932, the value of the Japanese whale catch was about one-third less
than in 1930; in terms of the number of whales caught, however, the deolime
was very mall. From 1932 to 1939, both the number and value of whales
caught inoreased moderately but steadily eaoh year.
An analysis of the number of whales caught in Japanese and colonial
waters shows that the number of whales caught in Japanese waters inoreased
60 peroent (from 1,368 to 2,153) during the period 1930-39, while the mmber
caught in colonial waters declined about 40 percent (from 349 to 145). Although
the number of whales oaught annually in colonial waters was very small as
compared with the number caught in Japanese waters, the value of the whales
caught in colonial waters was considerable. In the early l930's the mumber
of whales caught in oolonial waters was about 20 peroent of the mumber ceught
in Japanese waters, but the total value of the former was about one-half of
the value of the latter. In 1938 and 1939, when the number of whales caught
in colonial waters declined to less than 10 peroent of the number osught in
Japanese waters, the totel value of whales caught in colonial waters repre-
sented 20 percent of the total value of whales caught in Japanese waterse

II. WHALING IN ANTARCTIC WATERS
A. Development
Japanese whaling in Antarctic waters using mother ships with auiliary
ships or chasers began in 1934 when the Nippon Whaling Company, Ino., purehased
a mother ship and three chasers from Norway. Although Japan was some thirty
years late in entering into the Antarotio area, the operations of this compamy
and others that subsequently entered the field were markedly successful. Only
one mother ship was sent to the Antarotio waters in 1934-35, but by 1988, there
were six, aggregating a total of over 100,000 tons. Auxiliary ships or chasers
increased from 3 in 1934 to 49 in 1938. The number of crew members rose from
213 to nearly 2,800 during the same period (Table 2). In 1939 and 1940, the
Antarotic whaling fleet of the Japanese was probably further augmented in line
with Japan's announced intentions of inoreasing its share in : these waters.

B. Prospects for the Future
Antarctic whaling offers the possibility of providing either oil for
consumption in Japan or foreign exchange thibugh whale oil sales abroad. Both
oil and foreign emohange will be very scarce in Japan in the coming years. The
Japanese share of future Antarctic whaling will depend upon Allied policy.
Unless severe restrictions are placed on the number of shipe that the Japanese
are allowed to operate, on the area in which the Japanese are permitted to con-
duct whaling operations, or on the size of the whale catch, Antarotic whaling
may be of considerable economic significance to the Japanese in the future.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
Diplomatic Section
The Diplomatic Section of General Headquarters, Supreme Gommemder
for the Allied Powers, presents its compliments to the United Kingdem
Liaison Mission in Japan and has the honor to refer to the memorandum
for the Japanese Government (SCAPIN 1103), August 6, 1946, which
authorized the Japanese whaling operations in the Antarctic for the
1946-47 season.
As the Mission is aware, the SCAPIN, among other things. provides:
"Japanese whaling operations in the Antaretic are hereby
authorized for the 1946 - 1947 season. Such operations will be
conducted only in that part of the Pacific and Indian Oeeans of
the Antarctic bounded by south latitude 55 degrees on the north
and that part of the Antarctic land mass on the south generally
known as Wilkes Land, South Victoria land and the Ross Shelf
and between east longitude 90 degrees on the east of Greenwich
and west longitude 170 degrees on the west of Greenwich, herein-
after referred to as the Antarctic Whaling Area, during the
period and under the conditions set forth by the International
Whaling Conventions or modifications thereof which may be
adopted prior to the season.
rticle 2 of the Protocol of November 26, 1945. provides for noti-
fication to the Government of the United Kingdom of all whaling
activities. Although the British Government has already been informally
apprised of the Japanese expedition, it is requested that the Misd on
be good enough to forward formal notification thereof to His Majesty's
Government.
