=== Page 308 === (confidence 0.97)
TO
Informal Interagency Committee en Japanese
DATE:
Fishing and Marine Industry.
FROM
IR. - Nr. PLO S
SUBJECT:
Policy with respect to Japanese fishing.
The Committee held a meeting on February 11, 1947, at 3:00 p.m..
in Mr. Plory's office, for further consideration of the U.S. pesition
with respect to Japanese fishing policy. This meeting was called as
a consequence of developments at the last meeting of Committee 2, of
the Far Bastern Commission, February 6, 1947, when all members of
Committee 2 except the member for the U.S. expressed support of the
Australian proposal that the fisheries policy directive under which
628/2
Scap is currently operating be revised to specify a limited aree in
which the Japanese may conduct fishing and whaling operations, and to
provide that Japenese whaling operations be restricted to such ares.
The U.s. had submitted a counterproposal which would provide, in brief,
that subject to security provisions and Japanese adherence to
conservation measures, the areas where the Japanese should be permitted
to fish be a matter for the administrative discretion of scap, exmept
that the Japanese should not fish in waters within 50 miles of areas
vT
under the jurisdiction of foreign governments without the consent of
such governments. The U.S. member of Committee 2 agreed that the U.8.
would further consider the possibility of consent to a limitation of
area, but indicated that any such area would probably be considerably
larger than the area proposed by Australia.
Following is the substance of the discussion at the meeting of
the informal interagency comnittee:
Mr. Flory stated that the current controversy in the TEC points up
two conflicting philosophies with respect to the future of Jepan. On
the one hand there is the view, as expressed by most members of the mC.
that the Japanese should be rather rigidly controlled and their economic
activities restricted. This view arises in part out of psyahologieal
fears engendered by Japanese activities in the pre-war period, by their
disregard for the conservation policies and the rights of other nations,
by their utilisation of fishing operations as a cloak for spying activities
894.628
and other preparations for war; in part out of a fear of economic
competition from the Japanese in the future, a fear that they may again
flood the world with cheaply-made products. On the other hand is the view
that the Japanese must be permitted such a degree of economic activity
as to enable them to feed themselves, to maintain a standard of living
that will at least be high enough not to constitute an urge to foreig
aggression, and eventually to take their place in the family of nations.
It is
-1449
JAN 15
FILHD
5
3

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.99)
-a
It is the latter view which has formed the basis of our think
in the formmlation of the existing fisheries policy paper, mo-oss
and our counterproposal to the Australian paper. It is eur feeling
that the fact that Japan is a defeated nation should met be the
determining factor in decisions relating to her future economic devele
The establishment of a 50-mile sone around foreign countries in which
Japanese might not fish without specific consent of the coastal state.
suggested by the U.S. counterproposal as an interim measure to aveid
possible armed conflicts between the coastal and Japanese fishermem.
As has been repeatedly stated, the existing fisheries policy
directive, and any new directives that FE0 may agree upon, are only
interim measures, pending the final decisions to be made at the peace
conference. But interim measures unavoidably are construed as precedents.
Our consent now to a limitation of fishing areas and a restriction of
whaling activity would constitute a capitulation to the philosophy of
restriction -- a temporary capitulation perhaps, but nevertheless a step
which would make that much more difficult an eventual agreement on a
more liberal policy toward the Japanese economy. Noreover, any restriction
on Japanese fishing areas should be considered only in relation to the
total of economic activity to be permitted the Japanese. It may well
be (as the U.K. member of the FEC committee has suggested). that an
expansion of the fishing industry, in compensation for other industries
to be disallowed, would constitute less of a threat to the security or
to the economies of other nations than an expansion of other industries
that may be permitted to the Japanese.
Mr. Flory felt that the present situation might be summed up in
these two questions: Are we going to bring out these fundamental policy
differences now, and fight them through to the bitter end? Or should
we work out each situation on an ad hoc basis, waiting until the peace
conference to argue the general policy?
Other members of the committee suggested that this raised another
question: Would it be preferable for the U.S. to "hold out" on this
present question before the FEC (which is already torn by other
controversies on which agreement has not been reached), or to attempt
some form of compromise in the interest of international cooperation and
good will? Along the lines of the latter alternative, Mr. Bowman suggested
the possibility of agreeing to that part of the Australian paper which
proposes a limited fishing area, but resisting the move to restrict
pelagio whaling. Mr. Van Sant suggested that, since fishing was probably
of more vital importance than whaling to the Japanese economy, it might
be preferable to press for continuance of the existing directive, but
agree to a proviso that any future Japanese whaling activities in the
Antarctic be subject to TEC approval.
Mr. Allison felt that the security factor might be taken care of by
limitation on the size of Japanese vessels, and all agreed that it would
be useful to inquire as to the direction of the thinking of the Shipping
Division on this question. Mr. Allison also suggested that it would
be helpful

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.95)
be helpful to disouss this question with Mr. George Atehes
Adviser to sdar, who is presemtly in Washington. The members
ing the Departments of Commerce, Interier, and Agrioulture state
there would probably be within their Departments pressures for th
restrictive policy, but they felt that there might possibly be ov
concurrence in the broader view.
It was agreed that Mr. Flory would prepare a summation of the vis
discussed at the meeting, for circulation to the members before furthei
action is taken.
It is suggested that another meeting of the informal interagemey
committee be held on February 19 or 20. In the meantime the committee
members may wish to consult with other officers in their Departments
on this subject.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
CONFIDENTIAL
DATE: Pebruary 26, 19
SUBJECT:
Policy with respect to Japanese fishing.
PARTICIPANTS: Members of Informal Interagency Committee on Japanese Fishing
and Marine Industry.
/2-2647
COPIES TO:
IR - Mr. Kennedy
Interior - Dr. Deason
JA - Mr. Allison
Agriculture - Mr. Tyson
JK - Mr. Bowman
Commerce - Miss Bayles
ITP - Mrs. Haaga
Tariff Commission - Mr. Carter
ri
The Informal Committee met on February 26 in Mr. Flory's office, to
consider further the position the United States should take with respect
to the current discussion of Japanese fishing policy in the Far Eastern
Commission. (For report of previous meeting, see memorandum of Mr. Flory
to members of the Informal Committee, February 14, 1947.)
With regard to the economic aspects of the problem, Mr. Flory
stated that there appeared to be no reason to depart from the present
policy, as set forth in FEC-035. On the security aspect, he thought
that ScAp's interpretation of "neaf" as 12 miles (in plans for the
Antarctic whaling expedition) might raise some difficulties. Mr. Allison
reported that representatives of certain other countries with mmberahip
on FEC had informally admitted that emotion is a considerable elemeut in
attitudes toward security. Mr. Van Sant reported on conversations with
an officer of the Shipping Division. No conclusions have been reached on
a general policy as to size of the merchant marine to be permitted Japan
nor as to sise of ships. A sise limit of 5,000 tons has been disoussed,
but this is only tentative. The committee agreed that the shipping poliey
was as yet insufficiently clear to constitute a basis for conclusions on
the problem of fishing policy.
Mr. Allison and Mr. Bowman felt that a settlement of the current
controversy in FEC at this time would be desirable. The rest of the
JUL 141947
DTP Un
committes
PILED
Ehe

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
committee agreed on this point, and the following statement mas appreveds
"The basic policy with respect to fishing and aquatie indaskries
in Japan, as set forth in FEC-035, should continue in foree, exsept
as follows:
'Japanese whaling operations should not be authorised out-
side the area prescribed in SCAPIN 1033, June 22, 1946, withous
prior consultation with, and approval by, the Far Eastern
Commission.'"
It was agreed that, at tomorrow's meeting of Committee 2 of the FEC,
Mr. Barnett, the U.S. member of the Committee, should make an imformal
statement and ask the view of the other members, informally, as to the
possibility of the above statement as a basis for agreement. It was
agreed that no basis for this compromise should be stated other than
"to show our good will".

Japanese Fishing Policy.

Mr. Bowman called to inform me that Major Plimsoll, Australian
member of Committee 2, FEC, had called Mr. Barnett, US member of the
same Committee. Mejor Plimsoll asked if the proposal made in yes-
terday's meeting was, in Mr. Barnett's informal opinion, as far as
the US would go in the way of compromise. Mr. Barnett stated,
informally, that he felt that such was the case. Major Plimsoll
then stated that he personally was not eager to press the case, but
that he was under pressure from Canberra. However, he was reluctant
to have the controversy moved "upstairs". He thought that possibly
he c∞uld get agreement from Canborra if he and Mr. Barnott could
have a "gentleman's agreement" that, before any further extension of
the presently authorized Japanese fishing area (SCAPIN 1O33) were per-
mitted. SCAP would consult with the Allied Council for Japan.
Mr. Bowman felt that this would not be "giving away" any of
SCAP's prerogatives. I mentioned the Political Adviser's despatch
no. 481, July 1, 1946, which reports that, before the issuance of
689
SCAPIN 1O33 (the last extension of the fishing area) SCAP did consult
the Allied Council. Thus a "gentleman's agreement" for such con-
94.628
sultation in future would appear not to be inconsistent with SCaPt
practice in the past.

Office Memorandum· UNITED STAT
AH
TO
H - General Hilldrin
DATE: March 18.
FROM
SUBJECT:
Japanese Hishing Araks.
There has been going on in FEC for some time a discussion concerning
Japanese fishing and whaling, in which in general the U.S. has stood alone
against the other members who have wished to restrict severely the areas
which will be open to the Japanese. Furthermore, the present Japanese
Antarctic Whaling Expedition has evoked strong protest. SCaP has been
informed of all these discussions. In #28 of 7 Feb., SCAP's Political
Adviser pressed for allowing SCaP, as an administrative matter, to determine
the boundaries of areas open to Japanese fishing. In #47 of 5 March, SCAP
advised that a bilateral agreenent with China for the extension of areas
open to fishing had been reached and urged thet he be not restricted by the
necessity for submitting for FEC approval all such bilateral agreements.
The present FEC policy with regard to this matter is contained in
FEC-035 (SWNCC 99/3) of 7 November 1945. This in general provides that
the Japs may fish anywhere except that they cannot approach Allied territory.
SCAP gave effect to this policy in SCAPIN 1033 of 22 June 1946, in which he
specifically set out the areas authorized for Japanese fishing and whaling.
The enclosed cable advises SCAp that it appears probable now that
2
Committee #2 of FEC, the committee dealing with the fishing problem, will
be able to reach an agreement which will in effect retain FEc O35 as a
basic policy except that future Japanese whaling operations outside the
3-1847
prescribed area should not be undertaken without prior consultation with
and approval by FEC. This provision does not effect the present whaling
expedition. The cable goes on to advise that there is no necessity to sub-
mit bilateral agreements for FEC approval and asks SCAP's comments on the
proposed solution of the problem.
War Department concurrence for the enclosed draft cable has been obtained
through Major Miller (War Department extension 5031) and I recommend that you
approve the cable for dispatch through the War Department.
Attachments.
Phoned to Millu, (AD 747, asb.

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.98)
Office Memorandum · UNITED STATEs GOVE
To: A-H - General Hilldring
DATE: May 22, 1
FROM : FE - Mr. Allison
susject: Proposed Japanese Antarctic Whaling Expedition during l9
Season
PROBLEM
SCAP has requested authority to send a second SCAP-oprated
Japanese-manned whaling expedition to the Antarctic during the
coming season to consist of three factory vessels instead of the
two sent last season. Aide-memoires have been received from the
United Kingdom, Australia and New Zealand Governments, co-
signators with the United States of the international agreements
regulating whaling in the Antarotic, stating their unqualified
disapproval of any further Japanese whaling in the Antarotio
region. The question resolves itself into whether the material
gain which may be expected from the expedition is sufficient to
offset possible destruction of the international whaling agree-
ments and the international ill-feeling toward the United States
which would be incurred.
DISCUSSION
Legal Issues: It is the view of the Legal Adviser's Office
that Japan cannot as an occupied enemy nation appropriately adhere
to international agreements such as the Whaling Convention without
the coneent of all the signatory powers. Since three signatories
have indicated that they will not give thatassent there seems
no possibility of Japan's being admitted to a share of the Con-
ventional catch. A SCAP-operated Japanese-manned expedition
"under U.S. jurisdiction" with, as a minimum, U.S. masters and
Coast Guard inspectors could probably be authorized without tech-
nically violating the terms of the Convention, -- though possibly
not without compromising SCAP's role as Allied commander. However,
this device would never be willingly accepted by the protesting
Governments, and the undesirable political consequences and risk
of destruction of the Convention would be fully as great as if the
expedition had been authorized in open disregard of the Convention.
Probable Results of Authorizing Expedition:
1. It is highly likely, considering the present temper of
the nations parties to the international whaling agreements, that
those nations' whaling industries, which in 1945 generally opposed
limitation of the Antarctic cath to 16,Ooo blue whale units, would
be able to persuade their governments that they should not accopt
the as yet unratified protocol extending this limit to the 1947-
48 season, on the grounds that the United States action constitutes
a violation of the proposed agreement. Such action would open the
way
SECRET

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.99)
way to unlimited exploitation of Antarotic whale stocks and coul
lead, as has been the case in other areas, to the extinetion of
these stocks, nullifying this country's long efforts for their
conservation.
2. It may further be expected, if the U.s. authorizes the
expedition, that the interested countries will re-introduce into
the FEC a previous Australian paper which not only forbids Japamese
whaling in any waters, including those north of the equator around
the Bonin Islands, which are of real importance to Japan, but
forbids any extension of Japanese fiehing waters by SCAP without
previous FEC approval. The US could of course veto such a paper,
but it is questionable whether or not this would be politically
desirable when it 1s practically certain that we would have at
least eight of the ten other member nations aligned against us.
Representatives of the interested nations have informally made
clear that, should there be no Japanese whaling during the coming
season, they would be inclined to compromise on this Australian
paper so that Japanese whaling would be permitted in the Bonins
and that SCAP would have greater freedom in the extension of
boundaries of Japanese fishing waters. This might well be of
more importance to the Japanese economy than the continuation of
Antarctic whaling.
3. Authorization of the expedition by the Us would oreate
such a furor in the FEC that other important Us policies would
be likely to suffer.
Value of Expedition: It 1s anticipated that the expedition
would produce approximately eight million dollars worth of meat,
fats and oils, over half of which would be disposed of abroad for
foreign exchange to purchase other needed foods for Japan. However,
unless the Japanese catch should be authorized in excess of the
Conventional limit, with the very great disadvantages which this
action would entail, the net gain from the expedition will be
considerably less than eight million dollars in view of the fact
that: (1) Japan could use the veseels for whaling in other areas
(north of 40 degrees South Latitude) or for other fishing activities;
(2) the catch would be at the expense of the economies of Western
European whaling nations which the Us is trying to aid; (3) the
total consumable product from the 16,0o0 unit catch would be
reduced due to wasteful Japanese treating methods; and (4) there
is a reasonable prospect that the other interested governments
can be persuaded to make up the Japanese meat and oil deficiencies
which the expedition is intended to supply from their own catches
on a deferred payment basis, relieving the Us of this burden
without benefit of a Japanese expedition.
Future of Japanese Whaling: Failure to authorize the propesed
expedition would not in any way prejudice the long-range future of
Japanese
SECRET

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.97)
SECRET
- 3-
Japanese whaling. In its replies to the three notes thi de
could make it clear that ite decision to ban the expedition
attributable solely to the fact that Japan as an ocoupied nation
is not now in a position to accede io existing whaling agreements
without the other signatories' consent, and should in no sense
considered to imply modirieation of this nation's policy that Japan
position as a defeated nation should not be taken advantage of to
eliminate normal Japanese commercial competition. There is nothing
in the terms of existing whaling agreements to prevent Japan from
adhering to those agreements once the treaty with Japan is signed.
RECOMMENDATIONS
It is my belier in the li nt of the &bove considerations that
the US should not authorize the propoeed expedition. I do believe,
however, that in informing the interested Governments of this
decision they should be told that the US hopes and expects that
they will make available to SCAP on a dererred-payment basis from
their 1947-48 whaling catch amounts of meat and oil substantially
equal to those obtained from SCAP's 1946-47 expedition and retained
in Japan.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
MEMORANDUM OF CONVERSATION
RESTRICTED
May 27, 1947
SUBJECT:
1947-1948 Japanese Antarctic Whaling Expedition.
PARTICIPANTS: General Hilldring - A-H
Mr. F. C. Everson, First Secretary - British Embassy.
Mr. H. A. Graves, Counselor - British Embassy.
Mr. G. R. Powles, First Secretary - New Zealand Legation.
Major Plimsoll -'Australia
Mr. Knut Lykke - Counselor of Norwegian Embassy.
NA - Mr. Allison
IR - Mr. Flory
COPIES TO:
General Hilldring
JK
NA
BC
NOE
Dr. Deason, Fish and Wildlife Service, Department of
the Interior.
This Conference was called at 3:30 p.m. in General Hilldring's
office for the purpose of consultating on the above subject.
General Hilldring indicated that, in accordance with certain
commitments which had been made in connection with the 1946-1947
SCAP Antarctic whaling expedition, he was now consulting with re-
spect to a proposed 1947-48 expedition. General Hilldring said that
the views of his Government were as follows:
1. A SCAP whaling expedition in 1947-48 would provide food for
the Japanese occupation and foreign exchange, thereby decreasing the
obligations on the US taxpayers to supply Japan with an equivalent
amount of food and foreign exchange.
2. The expedition proposed would be a SCAP expedition, not a
Japanese expedition. This follows from the consideration that the
Supreme Commander has the responsibility for an effective occupation.
The question of rehabilitating the Japanese economy, per se, is not
a factor except as it contributes to effectiveness of the occupation
and to relieving the US taxpayers.
3. All the

=== Page 341 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
3. All the provisions of the whaling agreements will be care-
fully adhered to and the issue as it appears is basically one of
a conflict of interests between the US taxpayers and the Norwegian,
British and Australian whaling industries and economies.
4. The future of Japanese whaling after the occupation is not
involved in this question. He indicated, and all the representatives
present agreed, that any post-war limitations on Japanese whaling
would be decided in the peace treaty, not at the present time.
Virtually all of the arguments against Japanese whaling pre-
viously made by Australia, New Zealand, Norway and the United King-
dom were brought up at some time or another during the course of the
conversations. In addition the following points were urged:
1. Mr. Graves stated his belief that it would be hard for the
nations which are members of the international whaling conventions
to hold the Antarctic limit to 16,O0o blue whale units inasmuch as
it was anticipated, at the time the limitation was fixed, that there
would be no Japanese or German whaling. General Hilldring suggested
that the normal increase in the number of whaling expeditions would
contribute to the pressure for increasing the limit even though
Japanese and German expeditions were not involved.
2. Mr. Everson indicated that the British had cooperated fully
with the US in the furtherance of economic policies with respect
to Japan and they were prepared "to go along with almost any US
economic policy" but that they could not concur in a SCAP expedition
for 1947-1948.
3. Wajor Plimsoll stated that the Australians had cooperated
with the US in economic policy in Japan but that Australia objected
most vigorously to Japanese whaling. He pointed out that in the FEC
meeting all nations, except the United States, had concurred in oppos-
ing Japanese Antarctic whaling and that Australia had suspended pres-
sure for an FEC ban on Japanese Antarctic whaling on the personal
assurance of a US representative that the US was considering making
a counter-proposal which would ban SCAP Antarctic whaling. Major
Plimsoll urged most strongly the view that the US would lose much
more in terms of friendship with the allied powers by authorizing
Japanese whaling for 1947-48 than it would gain in dollars by for-
bidding such an expedition.
4. The Norwegian representative pointed out that the United
States had given considerable assistance to Norway in supply and in
reconstruction and that Japanese whaling would seriously affect the
reconstruction of the Norwegian economy and would sharply curtail
the amount of foreign exchange that it would have at its disposal.
5. Colone1

=== Page 342 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
5. Colonel Powles observed that in the pre-war period the
Japanese had operated their shore stations year round. He inquired
whether this was still the Japanese practice. Mr. Flory indicated
that a speciric inquiry on this point would be directed to sCAP.
6. Mr. Lykke inquired whether a report had been received on the
1946-1947 Antarctic expedition. Mr. Flory stated that no report
had as yet been received but that SCAP would, in due course, send to
Sandefjord the report which it is obligated to transmit under the
whaling agreements.
General Hilldring indicated that this consultation was of a
preliminary nature and stated that he would consult with these
representatives again before any final action was taken.

=== Page 343 === (confidence 0.96)
A-HH/mugu
DATE: July 24, 1947
FROM : RAF.
SUBJECT:
Attached Report of the Australian Observer on Whaling
Expedition.
The report makes fascinating and significant readng. If the
facts and figures given are true, as they appear to be, violations
of the whaling regulations regarding efficient and complete
utilization of the whales were on a much larger scale than BCAP,
with his tossing off of the matter of the "poor oil bearing bones",
has led us to believe.
The main trouble was that the vessel the inspector was on
had only half as many boilers as it should have had, and those
they had did not work well, with the result that the boilers
could not keep up with the number of whales brought in by the
catchers. Instead of catching fewer whales to accord with the
slow processing, the Japanese continued to catch as many as they
could, with the SCAP representative's blessing, throwing overboard
whole sides of ribs, backbones, etc., which they did not have
time to process, but getting a maximum production of meat. The
Australian states (page 5) that the Japanese knew the oil they
obtained would be allocated by the Allied Food Council while they
would be allowed to keep the meat, so they showed little reluctance
in throwing away oil bearing materials so long as they were
maximizing their meat take. This approach seems to have had th
full approval of the SCAP representative. The figures on pages 19
and 20 indicate that even allowing for the portion of the meat and
blubber salted for consumption instead of rendering into oil, the
vessel obtained 1,395 tons less oil than 1t should have for the
number of whales caught, securing 3,700 tons instead of 5,095.
I have marked the more important passages (on pages 3-5,
19-20, and 23, the rest being general description of how whales
are caught and processed) but strongly recommend reading of these
entire pages and, 1f possible, the entire report of 23 pages.
I have called Bullock of the Australian Embassy, who will let me
know whether SCAP has a copy of the report. Whether he does or
not I think we should cable him to ask if the picture in it is
correct and, if it is at all correct, instrut him to make
absolutely certain that no such violations and failures occur
HB
on the second expedition.
helened
agigna

=== Page 355 === (confidence 0.99)
TO
: NA - Mr. Fearey
DATE: 8eptember 22
FROM : IR - Mr. FloryNES
SUBJECT:
Comments on No. 1l88, United States Political Adviser
for Japan, dated July 25, 1947, as regards Australian
Observer's Report concerning 1946-47 Japanese whaling
operations.
In accordance with your telephone conversation of
September 9 with an officer of this Branch, I am attach-
ing a copy of a memorandum submitted at our request by
Dr. Remington Kellogg, Curator, Division of Mammals,
Smithsonian Institution, a recognized expert on whaling
matters.
The principal points made in Dr. Kellogg's memo-
randum are (1) discarding of bones on eighteen of seventy-
two days during 1946-47 Japanese Antarctic operations
constitute a violation of provisions of applicable
international whaling agreements, and (2) even includ-
ing hypothetical estimated oil production from salted
meat and blubber, oil production of Japanese expedi-
tions was "very low" in comparison with that of other
whaling countries. I would like to add the following
comments.
A statement of United States policy with respect
to fishing and aquatic industries in Japan, filed with
the FEC as FEC-O35, and dispatched to the Supreme Com-
mander for the Allied Powers for his guidance, includes
the following statement relative to whaling:
"d. Japanese fishing operations should con-
form strictly to:
(1) the provisions of agreements relating
to whaling to which the United States
is a party;"
From information received from SCAP by the Depart-
ment to date, it appears that a serious violation of the
provisions of applicable international agreements per-
taining

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
taining to whaling took place during the 1946-47 SCAP
authorized Antarctie operations of the Japanese.
Article 1l of the Agreement for the Regulation of
Whaling, signed at London June 8, 1937, and proclaimed
by the President of the United States May 18, 1938, which
applied to the 1946-47 whaling season, reads as foilows:
"The fullest possible use shall be made of
all whales tak en. Except in the case of whales
or parts of whales intended for human food or for
feeding animals, the oil shall be extracted by boil-
ing or otherwise from all blubber, meat (except the
meat of sperm whales) and bones other than the
internal organs, whale bone and flippers, of all
whales delivered to the factory ship or land
station."
In accordance with the above Article, all parts of
the whale must be processed except those specifically
intended for human or animal food, internal organs
whale bone (baleen) and flippers. Thus it is specified
that bones, such as those apparently admitted to have
been discarded on eighteen of seventy-two whaling days
during the 1946-47 Antarctic operations, must be pro-
cessed. As pointed out by Dr. Kellogg, the contradting
Governments, and consequently their whaling inspectors
have no power under the terms of existing international
whaling agreements to authorize acts in contravention
of regulations that are in force.P Articles 1 and 3 of
the 1937 London Agreement, which were in effect during
the 1946-47 season, read as fo1lows:
Article 1
The contracting Governments will take
appropriate measures to ensure the application
of the provisions of the present Agreement and
the punishment of infractions against the said
provisions, . . .
Article 3
Prosecutions for infractions against or contra-
ventions of the present Agreement and the regulations
made thereunder shall be instituted by the Govern-
ment or a Department of the Government.
The Department

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
The Department has received no information to dato
which would indicate that an appropriate investigation
has been made to determine whether a violation of
Article 11 of the 1937 London Agreement was committed
during the 1946-47 SCAP authorized Japanese Antarctic
operations, and that action has been taken under
Articles 1 and 3 of the same Agreement.
A careful examination of Dr. Kellogg's memor andum,
the Australian Observor's Report, and the comments of
the Natural Resources Section contained in No. 1188 from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, makes it
evident that the discarding of oil bearing bones during
the 1946-47 Antarctic operations contributed materially
to the low oil production of the Japanese expeditions,
indicating the seriousness of the violations.
I also note in the final reports on Japanese factory
ship operations in the Antarctic during the 1946-47
season, (C.L.0. No. 4152 (EA) dated May 30, 1947) which
was submitted to the International Bureau for Whaling
Statistics, that undersized whales were taken in viola-
tion of Article 5 of the 1937 London Agreement, but I
am not aware of appropriate action having been taken as
provided for under Articles 1, 3, and 13 (remuneration of
gunners) of the same Agreement.
In view of the prominent role played by the
United States in the development of agreements for the
conservation of this valuable international resource,
and also in view of the commitments made by this Govern-
ment to other interested nations to the effect that SCAP
authorized Japanese whaling operations would conform
strictly to the provisions of international whaling agree-
ments, the International Resources Division believes that
a failure by SCAP to do everything within his power to
comply fully in letter and spirit with the whaling agree-
ments would not only be highly embarrassing to this
Government but would pejudice the policies with respect
to Japanese fishing and whaling which we have strongly
supported in the face of vigorous foreign oppsition and
which ve hope to continue to support. Heweter this divi-
sion feels that it will be necessary to recommend that the
Department withdraw its support from the 1947-48 SCAP
authorized Japanese Antarctic expeditions and future SCAP
expeditions unless there is an indication that appropriate
action has or will be taken by the Supreme Commander with
regard

=== Page 358 === (confidence 0.96)
regard to violations committed during the 1946-47
season, and unless it appears that proper steps are
being taken to avoid the reptition of such violations
during the coming season.
Attachment:
Copy of memorandum from
Dr. Kellogg to Mr. Himbert,
September 8, 1947.
IR:ECastleman:MBM

Preliminary Study No 5
JAPAN'S BIG FISHING COMPANIES
SUMMARY
1. Japan produced 3,500,000 to B,000,000 metric tons of marine pro-
ducts annually fer several years prior to 1942.
2. About one-fifth of this amount was fish products from offshore
or overseas areas.
3. The offshore and overseas operations required expensive, highly-
mechanized fishing craft, gear, and processing machinery.
4. Almost all of Japan's overeeas fishing and most of the large-
scale, highly arganize? home coastal fishing was conducted by four large
fishing companies or their suusidiary ccmpanies. The operations of these
companies were often heavily subsidized by the Japanese Government.
5. The four big companies were:
a. The Nippon Suisan Kabushiki Kaisha (The Japan Marine Pro-
ducts Co, Ltd).
b. The Nichiro Gyogyo Kabushiki Kaisha (The Nichiro Fishing
Co. Lta).
c. Taiyo Gyogyo Kabushiki Kaisha (The Ocean Fishing Co).
d. The Kyokuyo Hogei Kabushiki Kaisha (The Polar Whaling Co, Ltd).
-1-

GEERAL HEADQUARTERS

Fatural Resources Sectien
Preliminary Study No & 1/
JAPAN'S BIG FISHING COMPANIES.
i. A. Intsoduction
1. Japan was the greatest fishing country in' the world. Before
1941 Japan's fisn catch accounted for 20-25 percent of the world's total
volume of marine products. It took.1,500,000 fishermen using 350,000
vessels to achieve this yield. About 20 percent of Japan's fishery pro-
ducts and nearly all of her exportable marine products came fram overseas
areas. almost all of her overseas fishery and most of the highly organ-
ized domestic fishery was in the hands of four large fishing companies
and their subsidiaries.
2. The largest of the four major fishing companies. in Japen and
perhaps the largest. fishing. company in the world was the Nippon Suisan
Kabushiki Kaisha (The Japan Marine Products Co, Ltd). 2/ This company
operated under an authorized capitalization of 94,262,000 prewar yen. In
1928 it was permitted to operate: 3/
87 percent of the total.trawling.
99 percent of the total crab processing in.floating
cannerics.
40 percent of the total deep-sea whaling.
76 percent of the total coastal whaling.
a. This company produced. 50 percent of Japan's. annual ice
output and ormed 6l percent of the refrigereting capecity. Twenty per-
cent of all exports of marine products were produced by the company.
b. The Nippon Suisan KK had subsidiary companies operating
in Argentina, Formosa, Borneo, the Philippines, Manchuria, and Korea.
1/ This stuty was prepared by Capt John L. Kask, Fisheries Division

c. The company operated fishing vessels and floating 
and processing factories in waters aear Kamchatka and in the Bering Ses op
per, in the East China Sea, the Yellow Sea, the South Chine Sea, the Bay
of Bengal, and the Indian Ocean, off Aastralia, on both the Pacific and
Atlantic sides of Central America, and off Argentian and other South Ameri-
can countries. It produced between 200,000 and 300,000 metric tons of
marine products a year.
d. The company was owned principally by the large financial
interests in Japan. More than one-half the prewar stock of 895,240 shares
was held by the president of the Manchuria Investment and Securities Cor-.
poration, a subsidiary of the Mitsui interests. The remaining stock was
held by rcprosentatives of other big interests. New stocks recently issued
have been sold to small shareholders.
e. The activities of this large organization were greatly
reduced by World War II. All of the Antarctic whaling factory vessels and
one-half of the remaining vesscls of the fishing fleet were destroyed, and
the prewar colonial and foreign cubsidiaries were severed. The company is
actively repairing old vessels end shore installations and constructing
new ones as permitted by tho Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
3. The second ranking fishing company is the Nichiro Gyogyo
Kabushiki Kaisha (The Nichiro Fishing Co, Ltd). 4/ This company was capi-
talized at 88,100,000 prewar yon. It was organized in 1914 and then, by
a series of amalgamations and purchases of ther companies. became the only
company to operatc in the leased areas of Soviet Kamchatka, and tho main
oporating company in Karafuto, the northern Kurils, and Hokkaido.
a. The company produced principally canned, smoked, and
salted salmon; salmon caviar; canned crab; and small amounts of other fish-
ery products. The company produced 225,060 metric tons of marine products
in 1941.
b. Besides its prccessing plants this company had extensive
cold storage holdings, boat-building and repair shops, machine shops, net
factories, and other facilities required for self-maintenance.
c. With the Soviet occupation of Karafuto and the Kurils
the operations of this iumense company came to a virtual standstill except
for small operations in northern Hokkaido. It engaged in the l946 herring
fishery near Hokkaido and in February 1946 was planning to enter the south-
ern trawl fishery. These recent operatiors, however, are small compared
to those of the prewar period.

4. The third big company is Taiyo Gyogyo. Kabushill Kaisha (The
Ocean Fishing Co 5/), better known in Japan by the name of the parent
company, the Hayashikane-Shoten. This company as of February 1946 was
capitalized at 60,000,000 prewar yen and was ovmed entirely by the Nakabe
family and a few close relatives. Seven of its 16 shareholders were named
Nakabe.
a. This company prior to World War II carried on extensive
fishing operations in Japan, Korea, and Formosa, besides whaling operations
at home and in the Antarctic. In addition to the actual output of fish
products, tho Taiyo Gyogyo kK or Hayashikane-Shoten Co had interests and
often controlling interests in a number of concerns allied to fishing.
xamples of side activities are boat-building and repair yards, specialty
products processing companies, canneries, cold storage and ice-making
plants, fish cake plants, and box and net making factories.
b. Hayashikane-Shoten, one of the first Japanese companies
to exploit the korean fisheries, had cxtcnsive operations there before
World War II. The company also had large holdings in Formosa, Manchuria,
and the South Scas.
c. Although its widospread foreign holdings are lost, this
company conducts extensive oporations around the Home Islands. Its fish-
ing flocts and shorc installations are well suited for activities in the
presontly authorizcd arca. Becausc high-levol decisions rested with one
men, tho company could makc spccdy adjustments to changing conditions.
It was thc most active producer of fish because of its independent nature
and adaptability.
5. Tho fourth company is the Kyokuyo Ho  ei KK (Tho Polar Whal-
ing Co). 6/ This company, capitalizcd at 20, 000,000 prowar yen, is the
smallost of the "Big Four". Its prewar operations included domestic and
Antarctic whaling and some trap nct fishing.
a. Tho physical propertics of the company included a large
cold storago plant and extensive oil storago tanks. Most of tho whaling
floct and all the oil storage tanks were destroyed during World War II.
b. The five subsidiaries, one of which was in China, wero
companies engaged in whaling and the processing of whalo by-products.

to a relatively low level. In 1945 only 78 whales

d. The principal shareholders 1n February 1946 were members

B. The Nippon Suisan Kabushiki Ka1sha (Japan Marine Products Co. Lt )
1. The Nippon Suisan Kk was the largest fishing company in Japan
and perhaps in the world.
2. The original company, the Kyodo Gyogyo KK (The United Fish-
ing Co, Ltd), was founded in 1913 with a capital of ¥2,000,000. This
company engaged in fishing with 25 trawlers. In 1934 the capitalisation
was increased to ¥10,000,000, and additional fishing activities were under.
taken. In 1536 the Nippon Godo Koxen KK (The Japan Consolidated Floating
Factory Co) and Nippon Hogei KK (Japan Whaling Co, Ltd) were taken over
by the Kyodo Gyogyo KK, and the capitalization was increased to ¥42,000,000.
In 1937 the firm acquired the entire business of Nippon Suisan KK (Japan
Marine Products Co, Lid), sellers of marine products. Capitalisation was
then ¥90,000,000. In March 1937 the Nippon Shokuryo Kogyo KK (The Japan
Food Industry Co, Ltd), an ice-making and cold storage company, was pur-
chased and the capitalization increased to ¥93,000,000. At this time the
name of the original company was changed from Kyodo Gyogyo Kk to the Nip-
pon Suisan Kk (The Japan Marine Products Co, Ltd).
3. In May 1942 the company was reorganized by government order
under the Marine Products Control Law. A new name, Nippon Gyogyo Tosei
KK (The Japan Ocean Fishery Control Co, Ltd) was aesigned. This company
was capitalized at ¥94,262,000. Its ice-making and cold storage facilities
were transferred to the Teikoku Suisan Tosei Kk (The Imperial Jish Pro-
ducts Holding Co), which was established in eccordance with the same law.
4. The Marine Pm ducts Control Law was abolished in October 1945.
The company was reorganized into a joint stock company and renamed Nippon
Suisan KK, its present name. As of February 1946 it had an authorized
capital of ¥94,262,000 and paid up capital of 69,512,000.

Memorandum of Conversation
Departigont of State
DATE: AprII 14, 194
British Attitude Toward Continuation of Japanese Whaling
SUBJECT:
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. F. C. Everson, British Embassy
John M. Allison, NA
COPIES TO:
FE
BO
JK/ Mr. Bowman
IR/ Mr. VanSant
4.628/4-1447
56218 fonkon
CONFIDENTIAL
At my request Mr. Everson came in to discuss the U.s.
suggested basis for resolving the impasse in Committee 2 of
the FEC on the question of the future of Japanese fishing
and whaling. I gave Mr. Everson the substance of SCAPs
views as outlined in Tokyo's telegram No. 69 of March 25th.
I made it clear that I was only discussing the matter
informally and was not committing the Department or the U.S.
Government to any definite line of action. I said I would
appreciate Mr. Everson's informal comment.
While agreeing that many of the points outlined by SCAP
seemed reasonable, lir. Everson said that unfortunately, accord-
ing to the latest indications he had received from London, the
British position was becoming fixed: that Japan should not be
CS/A
allowed to take part in Antarctic whaling in the future. He
said this wes based primarily on two grounds: 1) That the
limitation in the International Whaling Agreement on catch
of whales in the Antarctic to 15,O0o blue whale units would
probably be reached during the next whaling season without
Japanese participation. Should the Japanese be allowed to
894.628
participate, it would mean that the other whaling nations,
principally U.K., Norway and the Netherlands, would all have
to take a cut of what they otherwise would be able to obtain.
2) Japanese
m
Smll
gN
4-14
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 399 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 2 -
2) Japanese whaling practice in the past had been extremely
inefficient with the result that there was always a con-
siderable waste of whale meat and the British position is
that if the Japanese are allowed to whale, the total amount
of whale meat available will be less than it otherwise would
be.
I pointed out that under SCAP supervision the Japanese
were required to live up fully to the terms of the Inter=-
national Agreement and that with foreign inspectors aboard
it was probable that the amount of wastage had been con-
siderably reduced. Mr. Everson agreed that if definite
information on this point could be obtained it might modigy
to some extent the British position. However, he did feel
that it would be difficult to get around the argument concern-
ing the effect of the 15,000 blue whale unit limitation.
Mr. Everson emphasized that the British agreed with our
stand on the importance of fishing to the Japanese economy
and he said that he was sure that his Government would approve
of any plan which would permit SCAP to make bi-lateral agree-
ments extending the fishing area into the coastal waters of
other powers. He felt that perhaps progress might be made in
Committee 2 if the question of whaling could be divorced from
the general question of fishing, and that it might be well
to submit a paper dealing only with fishing which would oon-
tain a clause to the effect that the question of the future
of Japanese whaling would be considered seperately.
I thanked Mr. Everson for his observations and said that
I would discuss them with the interested officials in the
Department.
9g
NA: MAllison:mls
CONFIDENTIAL

Memorandum of/Conversation
DATE: May 1, 1947
SWNCC Vessels Reparation Policy - Japan.
SUBJECT:
PARTICIPANTS:Charles L. HOdges - JK
Mr. Flory - IR - Fisheries and Wildlife Branch.
COPIES TO:
Lr. Deason - Fish and Wildlife Service, Department of the Interior.
740.00119 Pw
Lir. Hodges called me to obtain IR's reaction to a SWNCC proposal
which would permit distribution of certain Japanese vessels as reparations.
The proposal was that all vessels over 3,Ooo light weight tons would be
x
made available for reparations immediately except that SCAp may retain
whatever vessels or groups of vessels deemed necessary for the needs of
the occupation.
CS/ V
Mr. Hodges explained that light weight tons was a classification
developed by the Navy to assure that the intent of the limitation might
not easily be circumvented.
Light weight tons signifies the weight of the vessel without ballast.
The gross tonnage, according to the Maritime Commission, ranges from 108
to 200 percent of the light weight tonnage. Other authorities estimate
light weight tonnage as 40 percent of the gross tonnage.
ir. HodgetOaplained when the question of Japanese floating canneries
and whale factory saips arose at the SWNCC meeting, the general reaction
was to insert a special exemption fcr such vessels. Mr. Hodges indicated
that it might be preferable to use the general escape clause, that is as
172
JAN 16 1913
I indicated
FIL
DOR- IP
m
OEPr    STTE
RECED
5

=== Page 410 === (confidence 0.96)
一2
I indicated that, based on what little I know of the temper
with respect to fisheries, it would appear to be better strategy
the general exemption since if FEC were to review a specifio fisl
exemption, it would precipitate in a new context many aspects of
fisheries questions which have been so difficult in recent FEC meetin
I reminded him that Australia and the USSR have already urged that the
large fishing and whaling vessels be made available for reparations
it is believed that other nations might view any proposed specifie
exemption for fisheries vessels with an eye to obtaining these vessels
for their owm countries rather than in the light of their necessity for
purposes of the occupation.

=== Page 421 === (confidence 0.98)
TO
: MA - Mr. Allison
DATE:
May 15,
PROM : IR - Mr. Flory WESF(mPh)
sUBJECT: Proposed Japanese Antarctic Whaling during 1947/48 Season.
The Protocol for the Regulation of Whaling signed at Washington
on December 2, 1946, will when notifications of acceptance have
been given to the Government of the United States of America by all
governments parties to the protocol of November 26, 1945, extend
to the 1947/48 whaling season the provisions of the November 26, 1945
protocol which supplements and modifies certain previous agreements.
Thus the over-all conservation catch limitation for Antarotic waters.
which the United States has championed, will contimue in offect
during the 1947/48 season.
The catch of the Japanese Antarctic expedition during the 1946/47 season
was included in the over-all catch limit for that season by the
absence of objection of the parties to the agreement, however notice
has now been served by certain parties that they do not intend to
894.628/5-1547
include further Antarctic catches by the Japanese within the conser-
vation limit.
Although it would be possible to carry on Japanese whaling in
the Antarctic under the jurisdiction of the United States of America
and thus force the acceptance of the Japanese catch within the over-
all conservation limit, other party nations would apparently very
strongly resent such action by this government.
Since the Protocol applicable for the 1947/48 whaling season has
not as yet been accepted by the signatory nations and since the
convention applicable for the 1948/49 and subsequent seasons has not
as yet been ratified, it is believed that although this country does
not at present whale in the Antarctic it would be best to maintain
as strongly as possible the cooperative attitude of the whaling
nations, thus protecting the over-all conservation catch limit and
consequently provide as effectively as possible for the conservation
844.628
and development of the whale stocks.
60
IR:ECastleman:bp

=== Page 425 === (confidence 0.98)
TO
: A-H, General Hilldring
DATB: May 16, 1947
suject: Proposed Japaneße Antarctic Whaling Expedition during 1947-48
Season.
SECRET
PROBLEM: SCAP has requested authority to send a second
SCAP-operated Japanese-manned whaling expedition to the Antarotio
during the coming season to consist of three ships instead of the
two sent during the past season. Aide memoires have been received
from the United Kingdom, Australia and New Zealand Governments
stating their unqualified disapproval of any further Japanese
whaling in the Antarctic. These nations are co-signators with the
United States of various international whaling agreements and pro-
tocols which regulate whaling in the Antarctic. It is question-
able whether the United States may authorize a Japanese whaling
894.
expedition in the face of opposition from co-signators to the
international whaling conventions without violating in letter and
in spirit these conventions. On the other hand, if the expedition
is authorized, it is estimated that it would contribute substan-
628/5
tially to Japanese economy not only by providing meat, scarce fats
and oils, but also an appreciable amount (between six and ight
million dollars) of needed foreign exchange with which additional
food could be purchased for the Japanese. The question resolves
itself into whether the material gain which may be expected from
the expedition is sufficient to offset the bad international
1647
feeling toward the US which will be incurred.
LEGAL ISSUE: The most recently expressed opinion of the
officials in IR who are directly concerned with the interna-
tional whaling agreements is that it would probably be possible
to find some legal grounds by which the United States could au-
thorize this expedition without technically breaking any inter-
national commitments. This would probably mean that the whaling
expedition would have to be considered as "under the jurisdiction
of the United States". While this is probably technically possi-
ble, it raises definite political questions as to its advisability
However, a copy of this memorandum is being presented to the Legal
S
Adviser's ofrice for an urgent definitive opinion on this point.
PROBABLE RESULTS OF AUTHORIZING EXPEDITION:
C
S
1. The United States has been the leader in international 
the catch of whales in order that their rapid trend toward ax
tinction may be halted. The US has adopted this policy becousg
505
of
SECRET

=== Page 426 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-2-
of (a) its general over-all policy favoring conservation and
(b) the increasingly renewed interest in the US fishing industry
in the possibilities of future US whaling expeditions. The Gov-
ernment has been concerned lest unlimited taking of whales might
lead to a paucity of stocks when the US industry was ready to
re-enter the field. Over the opposition of the British and Nor-
wegian whaling industries the US succeeded in persuading the
British and Norwegian Governments to agree in 1945 to an over-all
limitation of annual catch to 16,00o blue whale units. This
limitation has been retained in subsequent whaling agreements and
protocols and is included in the protocol applicable to the next
whaling season which was signed on December 2, 1946, but whioh
has not yet been ratified by the signatory Governments. Three of
those Governments have now stated that they would not be prepared
to agree to Japanese whaling even within the ceiling imposed by
international agreement. This means that, if the Us insists on a
Japanese whaling expedition, the signatory powers may well state
that they will not be bound to consider the Japanese catch as
coming within the 16,O00 unit limitation and that therefore the
whole purpose of the Us in fighting for conservation and the im-
position of a limit of catch will be vitiated. It 1s also possi-
ble that these countries would claim that the Us was not sincere
in its advocacy of over-all limitation and that they would there-
fore be obliged to respect the pleas of their own whaling indus-
tries and in future conventions set a higher limit or take off
the limitation altogether.
2. It is most likely that, if the US authorizes the Japanese
whaling expedition,the interested countries will re-introduce
into the FEC a previous Australian paper which not only forbide
Japanese whaling in any waters, including those north of the
equator around the Bonin Islands, which are of real importance
to Japan, but forbids any extension of Japanese fishing waters
by SCAP without previous FEC approval. The US could of course
veto such a paper, but it is questionable whether or not this
would be politically desirable when it is practically certain
that we would have at least eight of the ten other member na-
tions aligned against us. Representatives of the interested
nations heve informally made clear that, should there be no
Japanese whaling during the coming season, they would be in-
clined to compromise on this Australian paper so that Japanese
whaling would be permitted in the Bonins and that SCAP would
have greater freedom in the extension of boundaries of Japanese
fishing waters. This might well be of more importance to the
Japanese economy than the continuation of Antarctic whaling.
3. It is certain that the authorization by the US of Japanese
whaling would create such a furor in the FEC that other important
US policies might well suffer.
FUTURE
SECRET

=== Page 427 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-3-
FUTURE OF JAPANESE WHALING: An agreement not to authorize
a Japanese whaling expedition this year does not in any way
prejudice the long-term future of Japan in whaling. Once a
treaty is signed with Japan and it resumes its position as a
sovereign nation it may, in accordance with present terms of
the international whaling agreements, adhere to those agreements
at any time and may then conduct such whaling operations as it
desires. There is no provision in the whaling agreements by
which the present signators may prevent any country which wishes
from acceding to those agreements. The US policy that Japan'
position as a defeated enemy should not be used by the victors
to gain economic and trade advantages at the expense of Japan
need not be compromised in any respect by prohibiting a Japa-
nese whaling expedition this year. We can make a definite
statement of our policy in any replies to the three notes re-
ceived and can base our agreement to ban an expedition this
year on the grounds that Japan, being an occupied power, is
not now in a position to accede to the international whaling
agreements without the consent of the signatory nations and
that therefore until Japan is able to accede to the agreements
after the signing of a peace treaty she will not be permitted
to whale without the consent of the interested powers.
RECOMMENDATIONS: In v1ew of the above considerations it
1s my belief that the US should not authorize the Japanese
whaling expedition for the 1947-48 season. I do believe,
however, that in informing the interested Governments of this
decision they should be told that the US would hope and expect
that they might make available to SCAp on a deferred-payment
basis from their 1947-48 whaling catch amounts of meat and o1l
substantially equal to those obtained from SCAp's 1946-47
expedition and retained in Japan. A draft cable to SCAP is
attached hereto.

EUR - John D. Hickerson
Proposed Japanese Antarctic Whaling Expedition
(1947-48 Season)
PROBLIM1 SCAP has requested authority to send a secend
Japanese Whaling Expedition under SCAP supervision to the
Antarctic during the coming season. It is proposed that this
expedition consist of three factory ships instead of the twe
sent during the past season. Aide-memoires have been reeeived
from the Governments of the United Kingdom, Australia and New
Zealand expressing their determined disapproval of any further
Japanese whaling in the Antarctic.
DISCUSSIOM: The United States is signatory of various
FW
international whaling agreements and protocols regulating
whaling in the Antarctic including an agreement limiting the
overall annual catch to 16,000 blue whale units, which limita-
tion will presumably be ratified for the coming season. In
view of our persistent efforts over a period of years to
conserve the whaling industry it would be impossible to authorise
a Japanese expedition to take a catch in addition to the 16,000
94.628
blue whale units. At the same time, there is no reason to
assume that Allied whalers scheduled to operate in the Antaretie
during the coming season will not take the maximum catch
authorized. Therefore, any participation by the Japanese could
not increase the total catch.
The British go farther and contend that Japanese whaling
is less efficient than that of the Allied countries and that,
Si
therefore, any Japanese whaling within the limit of 16,000
blue whale units would decrease, rather than increase, the tetal
whale products to be obtained.
Prior to the war Japanese used very little whale oil and
it seems only logical that whale oil should continue to flow
in the normal channels, 1.e., to northern European countries,
especially so long as a critical food situation exists there.
Presumably IkrC will continue to bear this in mind in making
allocation of whale oil.
It is my belief that the only advantages to be gained by
Japanese participation in whaling would be (a) to provide
employment for a mumber of Japanese and (b) to provide a source
of dollar exchange for Japan. In each case these advantages
would be at the expense of similar advantages to Allied countries
equally in need:

=== Page 429 === (confidence 0.87)
mations to question the geed faith of the Waited
(im view of the justifieatien given fer the lgsé/ay
expeditien) and weald mest eertainly bring abeut series

THE LEGAL ADVISER
May 22, 1947
TO:
Mr. Fahy
FROM:
Le/P - C. E. Snow
SUBJECT: Authority of SCAP to Direct an Antarctic Whaling Expedition
QUESTION: Has SCAP the authority, vis-à-vis the Far Eastern
Commission, to conduct a "SCAP-administered,
Japanese-manned" whaling expedition to the
Antarctic?
1. SCap's authority to take any action in Japan may spring
from any one of three sources: (a) His position as Supreme Com-
mander for the Allied Powers, and sole executive authority for
the Allied Powers in Japan; (b) An interim directive from the
894.628/5-2247
United States on an urgent matter not covered by a policy already
formulated by the Far Eastern Commission; (c) His power to implement
a directive prepared by the U.S. Government to express a policy
decision of the Far Eastern Commission. The question at issue is,
under which of the three heads should action of the nature mentioned
fall?
2. It seems to be best argued that SCAp's anthority to con-
duct such a whaling expedition is inherent in his position as
Supreme Commander. Paragraph 5 of the Terms of Reference for the
Allied Council (Agreement of Foreign Ministers at Moscow on Estab-
lishing Far Eastern Commission and Allied Council for Japan,
December 27, 1945) provides that the Supreme Commander will issue
all orders for the occupation and control of Japen. that "in
all cases action will be carried out under and through the Supreme
Commander, who is the gole executive muthority for the Allied Powere
in Japan"; that although he is to consult and advise with the
Council in advance of the issuance of orders, on matters of substance
AUG 191949
and the exigencies of the situation permitting, his decisions on these
FILED
mattere shall be controlling. This expression of SCap's complete
authority to issue orders for the occupation and control of Japan
is the culmination of a series of documents.
894.62
a. In the Potsdam Proclamation of July 26, 1945, it was
proclaimed that points in Japan designated by the Allies were to be
occupied, and that the occupying forces of the Allies would be
ithdrawn when the objectives had been accomplished. This document
establishes that it was to be an Allied occupation, not an occupatien
S
by the United States.
CRET
H
9
2247

=== Page 440 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-2
b. In Secretary Byrnes' reply, for the President, to
the Japanese offer of surrender, August 11, 1945, it was stated
that from the moment of surrender the authority of the Emperer
and the Japanese Government to rule the State should be subieet
to the Supreme Commander of the Allied Pomere, who would take
such stepe as he deems proper to effeotuate the surrender terme.
and that the armed forces of the Allied Powers would remain in
Japan until the purposes of the Potsdam Declaration were
achievod.
c. The Japanese Government was notified on August 14,
1945 that General of the Army Douglas MacArthur has been designated
as Supreme Commander of the Allied Powers.
d. The final paragraph of the Instrument of Surrender
of September 2, 1945, which was signed not only by Japan, but also
by nine Allied Powers, repeats the statement made by
Secretary Byrnes (par b above) as to the subjection of the Japanese
Government to SCaP, and that the Supreme Commander would take such
steps as he deems proper to effectuate the terms of surrender.
3. The function of the Far Eastern Commission, as contrasted
with SCAp's power, as sole executive authority for the Allied Powers
in Japan, to issue all orders for the occupation and control of
Japan, is to formulate the policies, principlee and standarde in
conformity with which the fulfillment by Japan of its obligations
under the Terms of Surrender may be accomplished. (Agreement of
Foreign Ministers at Moscow, December 27, 1945, Par. A, IIA 1).
In accordance with these policy decisions of the Commission, the
U. S. Government prepares directives to SCAP who is charged with
their implementation (Par. AIII 1). The United States Government,
however, is expressly empowered, "pending action by the Commission
whenever urgent matters arise not covered by policies already
formulated by the Commission," to issue interim directives to
SCAP (Par. AIII 3), which directives, on the request of any member,
may be subsequently reviewed by the Commission (Par. AII A2), and
if the Commission decides that any such directive should be modi-
fied, its decision must be regarded as a policy decision (Par. A III 2).
Accordingly, even if the question whether or not there shall be a
second whaling expedition be considered a question of polioy rather
than of administration, nevertheless if the matter be considered
urgent, and since the Far Eastern Commission has as yet formulated
no policy, the United States may proceed unilaterally by way of
interim directive to SCAP, to direct such an expedition, and then,
if necessary, to veto any modification of the directive on review.
4. Procodure
CCRET

=== Page 441 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
-3-
4. Procedure by interim U. S. directive, as suggested
in the previous paragraph, seems unnecessarily labored, however,
SCAP's fundamental power to issue all orders for the ocoupation
and control of Japan, includes the power to make policy decisions,
assuming, of course, that the Commission has formulated no poliey
to the contrary. This was apparently foreseen by the Foreign
Ministers at Moscow, who specirically provided for the review
by the Commission, of "any action taken by the Supreme Commander
involving policy decisions within the jurisdiction of the Commission"
(Par. A II, A 2), and that if the Commission decides that any such
action so reviewed shoubd be modified, its decision should be
regarded as a policy decision (Par. A III 2). Accordingly, even
if the whaling expedition involves a policy decision within the
jurisdiction of the Commission, nevertheless, since the Commission
has formulated no policy, SCAP may act in the premises, and wdthout
any directive from the United States.
5. It is clear that there has been no policy decision by
the Far Eastern Commission on the matter of Japanese whaling.
A copy of FE0*035/1, December 13, 1946, is appended, which is a
statement of policy proposed by the Australian representative,
but never adopted. It will be noted that the paper provides
Par. 3(c)7 that no Japanese pelagic whaling should be permitted
anywhere, and that the FEC will not authorise ewentual Japanese
re-entry into pelagic whaling without agreement being reached in
the international authority for whaling. Even this document,
however, does not purport to forbid the Supreme Commander from
conducting a whaling expedition.
6. It is concluded that, for the purpose of providing
food and oil needed for the successful occuaption of Japan, SCAP
has the power, as sole executive authority for the Allied Powers
in Japan, to order and administer a second Antarotic Whaling
expedition. Even if there are questions of policy in connection
with such an expedition, which would be within the jurisdiction of
the Commission, nevertheless, pending action by the Commission,
such questions can be resolved by SCAp, or if preferred as a
matter of U.S. policy, by an interim directive of the United
States Government.

Dr. Deason, Fish and Vildlife Service, Departtent of the Interier
This Conference was called at 3:30 p.m. in General Hilldring's office
for the purpose of consultating on the above subject.
General Hilldring indicated that, in accordance with certain commitments
which had been made in connection vith the 1946-1947 SCAP Antarctic whaling
expedition, he was now consulting with respect to a proposed 1947-1948
expedition. General Hilldring said that the views of his Government were
as follows:
8
1. A SCAP whaling expedition in 1947-1948 would provide food for the
Japanese occupation and foreign exchange, thereby decreasing the obligationa
on the US taxpayers to supply Japan with an equivalent amount of food and
foreign exahange.
2. The expedition proposed would be a SCAP expedition, not a Japanese
expedition. This follows from the consideration that the Supreme Commander
has the responsibility for an effective occupation. The question of
rehabilitating the Japanese economy, per se, is not a factor except  it
894.6
contributes to effectiveness of the occupation and to relieving the 
taxpayers.
DCR O
3. All the provisions of the whaling agreements will be carefilly
adhered to and the issue as it appears is basically one of a conflict of
2
whjaling industries and economies.
5-2747
4. The future
183

=== Page 449 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
4. The future of Japanese whaling after the occupation is not
involved in this question. He indicated, and all the representatives
present agreed, that any post-war limitations on Japanese whaling would
be decided in the peace treaty, not at the present time.
Virtually all of the arguments against Japanese whaling previously
made by Australia, New Zealand, Norway and the United Kingdom were brought
up at some time or another during the course of the conversations, In
addition the following points were urged:
1. ir. Graves stated his belief that it would be hard for the nations
which are members of the international whaling conventions to hold the
Antarctic limit to 16,000 blue whale units inasmuch as it was anticipated,
at the time the li...itation was fixed, that there would be no Japanese or
German whaling. General Hilldring suggested that the normal increase
in the number of whaling expeditions would contribute to the pressure for
increasing the limit even though Japanese and German expeditions were not
involved.
2. r. Everson indicated that the British had cooperated fully
with the US in the furtherance of economic policies with respect to Japan
and they were prepared "to go along with almost any US economic polioy"
but that they could not concur in a SCAP expedition for 1947-1948.
3. Major Plimsoll stated that the Australians had cooperated with
the US in economic policy in Japan but that Australia objected most
vigorously to Japanese whaling. He pointed out that in the FEC meeting
all nations, except the United States, had concurred in opposing Japanese
Antarctic whaling and that Australia had suspended pressure for an FEC
ban on Japanese Antarctic whaling on the personal assurance of a US
representative that the US was considering making a counter-proposal
which would ban SCAP Antarctic whaling. ajor Plimsoll urged most
strongly the view that the US would lose much more in terms of friendship
with the allied powers by authorizing Japanese whaling for 1947-1948 than
it would gain in dollars by forbidding such an expedition.
4. The Norwegian representative pointed out that the United States
had given considerable assistance to Norway in supply and in reconstruction
and that Japanese whaling would seriously affect the reconstruction of the
Norwegian economy and would sharply curtail the amount of foreign exchange
that it would have at its disposal.
5. Colonel Powles observed that in the pre-war period the Japanese
had operated their shore stations year round. He inquired whether this
was still the Japanese practice. Mr. Flory indicated that a specific
inquiry on

=== Page 450 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
inquiry on this point would be directed to SCAP.
6. lir. Lykke inquired whether a report had been received on the
1946-1947 Antarctic expedition. Mr. Flory stated that no report had
as yet been received but that SCAP would, in due course, send to
Sandefjord the report which it is obligated to tranamit under the
whaling agreements.
General Hilldring indicated that this consultation was of
preliminary nature and stated that he would consult with these
representatives again before any final action was taken.

=== Page 451 === (confidence 0.97)
To : A-H, General Hilldring
DATE: May 27, 1947
suject: Japanese Antarctic Whaling Expedition
It is suggested that the following points might be made in
informing the British, Australian, New Zealand and Norwegian
representatives of the U.S. decision to authorize a SCAp-operated
Japanese-manned Antarctic whaling expedition. These points are in
answer to the specific objections listed in the aide-mémoire from
the British Embassy, which is the most detailed one received.
1. The British contention that the Japanese should be
excluded from Antarctic whaling because otherwise the catch
of other countries would be diminished is unsound. This is
contrary to the established American policy that Japan's
position as a defeated enemy should not be used by the
victors to gain economic and trade advantages at the expense
of Japan. This is also contrary to the long standing Ameri-
can opposition to trade monopolies.
X
628/5-2747
2. No proof has been adduced that permitting Japanese
vessels to whale would decrease substantially the amount
of whale oil and other products available to the world, as
1s claimed by the British. According to SCAP a note from
the Australian mission in Japan dated May 6, 1947, states
that the Australian observer with the last Japanese whaling
expedition reported that the expedition was conducted in
accordance with the International Whaling Convention except
in one particular, which was "the discarding of certain
poor oii-bearing bones during processing, since the facili-
ties of the factory ship did not permit this to be done and
at the same time eneure a maximum recovery of whale meat."
SCAP has stated that the facilities of the factory ship in
question will be modified to permit necessary processing in
complete conformity with convention and he gives assurance
that the Japanese will be required to observe all interna-
tional regulations during the proposed expedition.

other wheling expeditions.
4. The U.S. Government does not understand the
statement of the British Government that the nations
which took part in the International Whaling Conference
in Washington in November 1946 would no be prepared to
agree to Japan's accession to the international whaling
agreements. According to these agreements any oountry
which accepts the obligations imposed by the agreement
may accede thereto.
5. The U.S. Government believes that the dispos1tion
as reparations of Japanese whaling factory ships would be
premature at this time and that this question should be
considered as part of the general problem of reparations
rather than separately.
6. The security argument advanced by the British 1s
not well founded. As was done last year, SCAP will 1mpose
security regi:lations on the whaling expedition which will
prevent it from entering watere of Allied nations, unless
vith their consent, and representatives of SCAP will ac-
company the expedition to see that no security regulations
are violated.  There will also be, as was done in the last
expedition, a screening of sll expedit ion personnel. The
mere fact thet the whaling vessels can possibly be adapted
to naval use is not believed to be significant at this time.
There is no possibility in the foreseeable future of Japan's
being a security threat to any Allied power and as long as
security controls are continued by the Allies such a threat
should be negligible.
There is one point which must be handled carefully, and that
is SCAp's desire to have three ships in the present expedition as
against two last year. when the interested powers last autumn
learned through reports to the FEC and otherwise that SCAP had
authorized the Japanese Government to convert a tanker into a
whale processing ship protests were made to the U.S. Government.
After an exchange of telegrams with SCAp a reply was made to
these protests on December 13, 1946, which stated "information
has been received from the Supreme Commander that the use of this
ship for Bonin Islands operations, for which it was intended, has
now been found to be impracticable. Consequently conversion
operations will not be continued, and, upon the completion of
the hull to a point where launching is possible, no further
construction

difficult to convince the other Allies that SCAP did not go ahead
and finish preparing a third whaling vessel in spite of assurances
given. I am merely calling this to your attention so that you may
decide what should be aone on this particular point.

Dear General Hilldring:
During the recent meeting in your office in
connection with the question of whaling expeditions from
Japan, Mr. Graves, Counselor of the British Embassy, stressed
the detrimental effect on the world supply of cils and fats
xR
which would result from Japanese participation in the
Antarctic whaling operations during the next season.
He explained thatthe permitted total catch
of 16.ooo blue whale units will easily be reached by the
expeditions from other countries and that the substantially
lower output of oil per blue whale unit obtained by
Japanese expeditions would cause a smaller supply of whale
oil. On that occasion the figures from last season were not
on hand, but I have now obtained the final figures reported
to the Committee for International Whaling Statistics.
The figures show that the output of oil per
blue whale unit on the SCAP expeditions was 78 1/2 barrels
while on the Norwegian expeditions taken together the output
was 116 1/2 barrels, which is 48 1/2 % better than the
Japanese. This Norwegian output is very close to the average
Norwegian output for the four last seasons before the war
2
The Japanese catch of 932 blue whale units,
processed as effectively as on the Norwegian factory hip
hould have yielded 35.416 barrels or 5.9o3 1ong tons more
than the actual. output of 12.2oo tons.
If the

=== Page 455 === (confidence 0.97)
NORWEGIAN EMBASSY
WASHINGTON 7. D. C.
-2-
If the Japanese expeditions were to opora
season and their catch is estimated at 12oo blue whale unit
out of the total of l6.ooo, it may be expected on the basis
of last season's processing that it would result in a loss
to the world supply of oils and fats of some 7.6oo tons
of whale oil.
Yours sincerely
K. Yylele

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
(TELEPHONE)
DATE: June 9, 1947
2
SUBJECT:
Crabmeat from Japan.
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. R. W. Tyson, Department of Agriculture
IR - Mr. VanSant
894.628/6-947
COPIES TO:
1-1000
Hr. Tyson called me to say that someone had informed him that a rumor
is going around in the industry that 400,000 cases of crabmeat will be
forthcoming from Japan. He asked me if we had heard of any more besides the
31,000 cases that have been discussed for some time. I said that the last
I had heard, the quantity was now down to 22,000 cases.
I called Mr. Claassem, USCC, but as he is out of town for the week, I
was referred to Mr. Parks (RrC ext. 848). Mr. Parks informed me that SCAP
has now withdrawn 12,000 cases from the offer (presumably having sold it
elsewhere) so that now USCC will have no more than nine or ten thousand
cases to offer - if that much. He knew of no other indication of crabmeat's
CS/A
being available for import from Japan.
I called Mr. Tyson back and gave him this information. He told me
that 400,000 cases was about the ennual U.S. importation of Japanese crabmeat
in the prewar period. He had told the "rumor-monger" that 400,000 was a
fantastic figure to expect from Japan now, because most of that came from
the Kanchatka area, from which the Japanese are now barred. Mr. Tyson stated
894.6
further that he thought he would talk with the National Canner's Association
and see if they couldn't put out some publicity to counteract this rumor.
