=== Page 460 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE:
June 10, 1947
Entrance of American fishing interests into the
SUBJECT:
Japanese fishing industry
PARTICIPANTS:
Iiss Farke, Assistant Director of Fisheries
Division, National Canners Association; and Mr.
Whi tman - JK
COPIES TO:
ESP - Mr. Martin
IR - Mr. Flory
NA
Miss Parke called at her request to discuss, on
behalf of National Canners Association, the possibility
for expansion of American fishing industry into Japanese
fishing. She stated that the American industry was con-
sidering the possibility of expanding abroad in order
to supply the U. S. markets with low cost fish products.
-1047
She wished to determine what were the possibilities in
Japan for establishment of canning or freezing plants,
the contracting of Japanese fishermen, etc. She also
wished to learn what the plans were as to the areas in
which Japan would be permitted to fish in the future.
I said that it was too early to oncourage substantial
American investments in Japan, but these must await settle-
ment of reparations and simiiar problems. I stated that
no comment could be made on the proposal for a U. S.
firm to contract Japanese fishermen without much more
definite ideas as to what the American industry had in
mind. I suggested that if the American industry wished
to start an early participation in Japanese fishing it
should go to Japan prepared to offer a market for Japanese
canned or frozen fish products and if possible working
capital in the way of tin plate, etc. In that way there
might

=== Page 461 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
might be an opportunity for,profit to the industry
while at the same time aidwthe Japanese fish recovery.
Miss Parke implied that the industry would wish, at
the very least, to send someone to Japan to explore
the possibilities but would perhaps not move very
quickly along the lines suggested. Apparently the
industry is less interested in tuna, increased output
of which could be obtained with comparatively little
political reaction, than it was in salmon or crab
which would involve USSR and the U. S. political
opposition.
At the end of the conference, in response to a
question by Miss Parke, I stated that it was contem-
plated that the U. S. generally would oppose placing
in the peace treaty limitations on Japanese economic
activities which are not clearly related to problems
of security.

NOTE
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
His Britannic Majesty's Ambassador presents his
compliments to the Secretary of State and has the honour
to inform him that His Majesty's Government in the United
THEAST ASIAN AFFA
Kingdom are studying the text of the Aide Memoire on
whaling which Mr. Hugh Borton handed to Mr. Everson on
the 9th June, 1947. In the meantime they protest strongly7
OF STATE
against the decision which, to their great concern, has
9
apparently been taken by the United States Government to
628/6-1747
permit the Supreme Comnander for the Allied Powers to
authorise a second Japanese Antarctic expedition, - a
decision which they find inconsistent with the undertakings
given by the United States Government to the other govern-
874.627/9-346
ments interested in Antarctic whaling.
JHFELET
2.
In an Aide Memoire dated the 4th October, 1946, the
State Department announced that the United States Govern-
ment was in complete accord with the view that any long-
range consideration of the rights of. Japan to conduct
whaling operations is a matter for Allied discussion and
decision. They added that the Supreme Commander had in-
S
formed the Department that no authorisation for any possible
V
future Japanese whaling expeditions in the Antarctic or
elsewhere would be given without prior consultation with
5
FILED
the interested governments.
i心
3.
The views of His Majesty's Government regarding
Japanese whaling were made known to the United States Govern-
ment when the first expedition was under discussion last
year and they were repeated in the Far Eastern Commission
and its conmittees. In February, 1947, the discussion of
as
policy/

=== Page 464 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
policy with regard to whaling and fishing in the Economic
Committee of the Commission was adjourned on the under-
standing that the United States member would put forward
fresh draft policies for Japanese fishing and whaling
respectively. No intimation that the United States Govern-
ment contemplated a second Antarctic whaling expedition
was received until members of the staff of the Embassy
were called to the State Department to hear General
Hilldring's oral statement on the 27th May. This was
shortly followed by the Aide Memoire of the 9th June which
states that it has been found advisable to authorise a
second expedition. His Majesty's Government fear there-
fore that the United States Government may have permitted
preparations for a second Antarctic expedition to go for-
ward without prior consultation with the interested govern-
ments, let alone an Allied decision. In these circumstances
His Majesty's Government must earnestly request that author-
isation for a second expedition be withheld from the Supreme
Commander or, if already issued, cancelled.
BRITISH EMBASSY,
WASHINGTON, D. C.,
17th June, 1947.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: June 18, 1947
SUBJECT:
Australian Use of Japanese Whaling Unit.
PARTICIPANTS:
Major J. Plimsoll, Australian Delegation to FEC
Mr. R. H. Whitman, JK
COPIES TO:
ESP - Mr. Martin
A-H - Mr. Lewis
8
NA - Mr. Allison
6
IR - Mr. Flory
1-1408
On the instructions of his Government, Major J. Plimsoll requested the
2
uniformal reaction of the U.S. Gevernment to an Australian proposal to
8
operate a Japanese whaling unit during the next whaling season. Australia
would agree to give to Japan the oil allocated by the IEFC and expect pay-
ment on the same terms as the U.S. took payment for the supplies furnished
by the U.S. Major Plimsoll stated that in this way it was hoped that a
large part of the U.S. argument for Japan whaling would be obviated.
I raised two points - first, would Australia still object if other
Japanese vessels continued to whale; second, what would happen to the
f
whale meat.  Major Plimsoll stated that other whaling vessels should be
offered to other Governments on the same terms and that Japan should not
7
whale at all. Major Plimsoll also said that probably less meat would be
taken than by a Japanese expedition but that what was taken would be made
available to Japan on the same basis as oil.
Major Plimsoll then asked whether the operation should be done on a
basis of taking the vessels as reparations or leasing them. I stated
that since there would be numerous claimants for the vessels as reparations
it appeared simpler to request vessels on a lease basis.
I did not inform Major Plimsoll of the final decision of the U.S. on
Japanese whaling and it is possible when this is announced, his Government
will loose interest. I think, however, some kind of an answer should be
given the Australian Government.

OFFICE OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
DATE: June 20
OF STATE D
SUBJECT: Japanese Second Antarotio Whaling Expedition
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. Knut Lykke,
Counselor of Norwegian Embassy,
and
Mr. Allison
COPIES TO:
NOE
IR
894.628/6-2047
JK
Mr. Lykke oalled at his request this morning and handed
1-1400
me the attached aide-mémoire presenting an official protest
from his Government against the decision of the United States
Government to authorize the second Antarotic whaling expedi-
tion under the control of SCAP.
Mr. Lykke said he wished to emphasize the statement in
the Norwegian aide-mémoire that his Government was not con-
vinced of the vital necessity of Antarotio whaling for the
purpose of supplying the Japanese population with proteins
and that the necessary proteins might more sconomically be
provided through an intensification of the Japanese fisheries.
I bold Mr. Lykke that I did not feel able to say any more
at this time than had been told him before, but that I would
pass on his remarks and copies of the aide-mémoire at once to
the interested American authorities.

=== Page 473 === (confidence 0.99)
NORWEGIAN EMBASSY
WASHINGTON 7. D. C.
AIDE MEMOIRE
The Department of State through an Aide Memoire informed
the Norwegian Embassy on June 9, 1947, that the United States
Government had authorized a second Antarctic whaling expedition
894.628/6-2047
under the control of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
in Japan, and gave the reasons for the decision.
The Aide Memoire has been studied by the Norwegian
Government, but has not changed it's opinion that whaling ex-
peditions from Japan should not be permitted to take part im
the Antarctic whaling operation. The Government has instructed
the Embassy to lodge an official protest against the decision
of the United States Government.
The Norwegian Government feels that Japan, because
of her expansionist and aggressive policies that led to her
894,628
dowmfall, should not for many years be permitted to oquip and
以
send out

=== Page 474 === (confidence 0.98)
NORWEGIAN EMBASSY
WASHINGTON 7. D. C.
- 2 -
send out on leng expeditions ships of the speed and range
necessary for modern whaling. Japan's reckless expansion in
the whaling field and her total disregard of conservation
principles was a serious impediment to the international
efforts for the protection of whales.
The Norwegian Government is of the opinion that a
reestablishment of the Japanese pelagic whaling industry would
represent a serious threat to the cooperation for the perpetua-
tion of whaling as a great natural resource, and that the steps
toward such reestablishment, taken with the sanction of the
occupying forces, should be discontinued.
The Norwegian authorities appreciate the difficulties
of the occupying forces in organizing the supply of the
Japanese population with food. They are not, however, con-
vinced of the vital necessity of Antarctic whaling for this
purpese. It has been suggested that the necessary proteins
might be provided more economically through an intensification
of the

=== Page 475 === (confidence 0.99)
NORWEGIAN EMBASSY
WASHINGTON 7. D. C.
-3-
of the Japanese fisheries. With regard to the sapply of fats
and oils,it is pointed out that the whale oil allocated to
Japan could be delivered by tanker directly from the Antarctie.
Past experience of ineffective oil yield on Japanese factory
ships, as well as reports of the results of last season's
operations make it highly probable that Japanese participation
in Antarctic whaling would entail a loss in the total output of
whale oil, as the total catch of sixteen thousand blue whale
units will easily be reached by the other expeditions.
The Norwegian Government hopes that the Government of
the United States will find it possible, in view of the
opposition expressed by all interested nations, to withdraw
it's consent to Japanese Antarctic whaling for the 1947/1948
season.
NORWEGIAN EMBASSY, WASHINGTON, D.C., June 20, 1947.

=== Page 482 === (confidence 0.96)
ATTE MOEMOTHE
The Australian Government learns with comoerm that the Suu
Commander for the Allied Powers has authorized a further Japanese whaling
expedition in the Antarctic in the 1947-48 season. The Australiam Goverm-
ment greatly regrets that this step should have been taken in face of
the strong protests of the Australian, New Zealand, Norwegiam, and United
894.628/6-2347
Kingdom Governments. The waters of the Antarctic are of vital concern
to the safety and welfare of Australia. and the Australian Govermment
continues to oppose the presence of Japanese in those waters before the
594.628
conclusion of a peace treaty with Japan.
The difficulties of the United States Government in financing
relief for Japen and the difficulties of the Supreme Commander in seouring
adequate supplies are fully appreciated. The Australian Govermment is
prepared to play its part in relieving those difficulties. To this end
it proposes that a factory ship and chasers should be made available to
CS
Australia immediately for use in the coming wheling season. The Aus-
tralian Government is in a position to man these ships and operate them
more efficiently than the Japanese, thus increasing the amount of oil
available. The allocation of the food products to relieve the Japanese
food shortage would be entirely a matter for determination by the Sap-
reme Commander, and the oil would be allocated as at presemt by the
International Food Emergeney Committee, thus assuring supplies at

=== Page 483 === (confidence 0.99)
2
least as adequate as those which would be obtained from the proposed
Japanese expedition. The Australian Government is prepered to consider
methods of payment which would avoid the cost being a charge on United
States revenue. If a separate Australian expedition is not considered
feasible or desirable, Australia is prepared to conduct the erpedition
in association with other Allies, in particular the United Kingdom, New
Zealand, and Norway.
The foregoing proposal was the subject of preliminary dis-
cussions by an officer of this Embassy on 17 June and again on 19 June
with Mr. R. H. Whitman, Associate Chief, Japanese and Korean Economic
Affairs Division. Mr. Whitman stated on the second occasion that the
proposal submitted at the first discussion was being actively studied
in various divisions of the State Department, and that the views of the
Department would be made known within a few days. The Australian Govern-
ment forebore to go into detail at this stage, since it was desired to
leave as much flexibility as possible so that full account could be
taken of the views and practical problems of the United States Government
and the Supreme Commander before a formal proposal in detail was submitted.
It is desired to protest most strongly at this authorization
and public announcement of a second Japanese whaling expedition at a
time when the Australian Government was making a sincere attempt to
formulate an alternative which would meet the position of both Govermments.
when a proposal of this nature had been made officially, and at a time

=== Page 484 === (confidence 0.99)
3
when it was apparently under active consideration in the State Depart
It is assumed that the action in authorizing a further Japanese expeditiom
was not intended to prejudice the consideration of the Australiam proposal
by the United States Government, and that the work now being undertakem
in fitting these ships would not necessarily prevent their transfer to
Australia if agreement on the Australian proposal can be reached. An
assurance to this effect would be welcomed.
The Australian Government attaches the greatest importance to
this question and is desirous of reaching an agreement that will pay full
attention to the interests of the United States and Australian Governments
and of other interested nations.
23fune 1947

My

21 June 1947
MEMORANDUM FOR: The Honorable
The Members of the Allied Counoil for
Japan.
SUBJECT:
Second SCAP-Controlled Antarotic Whaling
Expedition.
There is enclosed, for the information of the
Members of the Allied Council for Japan, a copy of a
Momorandum which will be issued to the Japanese
Government authorizing a Japanese-manned ScAP-controlled
whaling expedition to the Antarctic. Active preparations
for this expedition should have begun by 15 May 1947.
As the various Members are undoubtedly aware, the
question of this expedition has been under disoussion
in Washington between the United States Government and
other interested Governments. This authorization to the
Japanese Government is pursuant to instructions from the
United States Government.
GEORGE ATCHESON, Jr.
Deputy for the Supreme Commander.
Chairman and United States Member

Central Liaison Office, Tokyo
Japanese Whaling Operations in the
Antarctic in the 1947-1948 Season.
1. Reference is made to:
a. Memorandum for Japanese Government, from
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers, AG 800.217 (3 N0V 45) NR (SCAPIN 233), 3
November 1945, subject: "Whaling Industry".
b. Memorandum for the Japanese Government from
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers, AG 800.217 (24 Aug 46) NR (SCAPIN 1154), 24
August 1946, subject: "Whaling 1ndustry".
c. Memorandum from Japanese Government to
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers CLO No. 1922 (EA) 20 march 1947, subject:
"Japanese Whaling Operations in the Antarctic for the
1947-1948 Season".
2. Japanese whaling operations in the Antarotic
are hereby authorized for the 1947-1948 season. Such
operations will be conducted only in that part of the
Pacific and Indian Uceans of the Antarctic bounded by
south latitude 55 degrees on the north and that part
of the Antarctic land mass on the south generally known
as Wilkes Land, South victoria Land and the Ross Shelf
and between east longitude 90 degrees on the east of
Greenwich and west longitude 170 degrees on the west
of Greenwich, hereinafter referred to as the Antarotic
Whaling Area, during the period and under the conditions
set forth by the International Whaling Conventions or
modifications thereof which may be adopted prior to the
season.
3. Within thirty days of the receipt of this
memorandum, the Japanese Government will submit to the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers for approval,
a plan for the organization and operation of the
Antarctic Whaling Expedition. The plan will include
a list of the names and gross tonnage of two factory
ships and the accompanying catcher boats and transports
to

=== Page 499 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's No. "1135
June 25, 1947
-3-
to be used in the Antarctic Whaling Area. Subsequent
to the approval of the plan, or modifications thereof,
any substitution of vessels for those named must be
approved by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
4. Vessels participating in Antarctic whaling will
comply with the following:
a. Each vessel will be marked and operated in
conformity with directives from the Administrator, Naval
Shipping Control Authority.
b. Each vessel will display in the presoribed
manner only the International code flag "E" (Easy), ex-
cept that a vessel operating within the authorized
Japanese fishing area will fly in addition the flag of
the Japanese Merchant Marine.
c. The master of each of the participating
factory ships during the periods enroute to and from the
Antarctic Whaling Area will forward a daily radiogram to
the Japanese Bureau of Fisheries in Tokyo listing the
location of his ship at 1200 hours on the date the radio-
gram is dispatched.
d. The master of each of the participating
factory ships during the whaling operations in the author-
ized Antarctic Whaling Area will forward a daily radio-
gram to the Japanese Bureau of Fisheries in Tokyo listing
the following information:
(1) Location of his ship at 1200 hours
on the date radiogram is dispatohed.
(2)
Number and kind of whales processed
on his ship during the previous 24-
hour period ending at l200 hours and
output of whale oil, meat, blubber,
and other products.
e. Application for frequencies for radio com-
munications by the whaling fleet will be made to Civil
Communications Section, General Headquarters, Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers.
f. Each vessel will proceed to and return from
the Antarctic Whaling Area by the most direct route and
without approaching any land nearer than 12 miles except
when passing through the Lombok Straits, where continuous
passage must be maintained. The route to be followed by
each vessel to and from the Antarctic Whaling Area will
be supplied by the Japanese Government to the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers and the Commander, Naval
Forces Far East, 15 days prior to departure of each vessel
from Japanese ports and from the Antarctic Whaling Area.
g. The

=== Page 500 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's No. 1135
June 23, 1947
-4-
g. The master of each of the factory ships on
departing from the Antarotic Whaling Area will dispatch
a radiogram to the Japanese Bureau of Fisheries in Tokyo
stating date of departure.
5. The Japanese Bureau of Fisheries will maintain
at its office in Tokyo a file of all radiograms roquired
to be dispatched in accordance with paragraph 4 for
reference by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
when desired.
6. The whale oil produced by operations in the
Antarotic Whaling rea will be transported to Japan and
as soon as possible after arrival the oil will be suit-
ably stored and safely guarded. Within ten days of
storage, the Japanese Government will notify the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers of the location of the
place or places of storage and the amount stored. Final
disposition of the whale oil will be the subject of a
further memorandum from the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers to the Japanese Government.
7. The attention of the Japanese Government is
directed to memorandum on the whaling industry referred
to in paragraph 1 a, above. Reports called for in accord-
ance with referenced memorandum will be subpitted to the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers by 1 June 1948.
8. This authorization does not establish any pre-
cedent for whaling operations in the Antarotic area or
in any other area for any subsequent period of time;
nor is it an expression of Allied policy relative to
ultimate determination of national jurisdiction, inter-
national boundaries or fishing and whaling operations in
the area concerned, or in any other area.
FOR THE SUPREME COMMANDER:

from the United States Political Adviser for Japen, Tekye

COPY
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
FAR EAST COMMAND
Public Information Office
Immediate Release:
22 June 1947
A second SCAP-controlled and Japanese-manned Antarotio
whaling expedition has been authorized by the Supreme
Commander pursuant to instructions from the United States
Government.
The reasons for this action are obvious. The last
whaling expedition produced for Japanese consumption some
21,000 metric tons of needed protein foods which helped
make up the food deficit in Japan. It also produced for
the world market, which is in short supply, over l2,000
tons of whale oil and 1l tons of Vitamin A and D oil. It
is expected that the second expedition will produce an
equivalent amount of whale meat for consumption in Japan
and an equivalent amount of needed oils for allocation to
other parts of the world by the International Emergeney
Food Council.
There is a continuing food shortage in Japan. The
burden of supplying the deficit imports of food continues
to fall upon the United States, which is also endeavoring
to meet demands for food from many other countries. With
their whaling fleets and trained crews the Japanese possess
a means of providing additional food for themselves and of
helping to meet a world deficit in oils. These utilities
at this moment of need must be used.
Not to do so would mean a direct cost to the United
States of over U.S. $10,000,000, the equivalent of approxi-
mately 40,000 tons of wheat and an additional loss of
approximately (T.S. $6,000,000 in foreign exchange from the
sale of whale il. There was no Antarotic whaling during
the war and the supply of whales there has accordingly
increased so that no undue diminution of the whaling
potential is involved.
The second erpedition, as was the case with the first,
is being organized under the immediate supervision of
Colonel Hubert G. Sohenck, Chief of SCAP's Natural
Resources Section. Allied observers will supervise as
before. The Antarctic waters in which the whaling is to
be conducted are international. The SCaP observers
accompanying the vessels will submit full radio reports
daily and will require implicit observance of inter-
national whaling regulations.
As SCAP-controlled Japanese manned ships operating
to repatriate 5-1/2 million Japanese prisoners of war and
civilians have during the past 18 months entered numerous
Allied ports in the West and Southwest Pacific without
incident, it is believed that no problem of seourity is
involved in this second whaling expedition.

AIDELBNOIIB
The New Zealand Government has Learned With
regret of the unilateral deciaion of the United States Geve
permitting the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers in Japen
to authorise a second Japanese Antarctic Whaling Expedition to
operate during the 1947-48 season. It is a matter of deep
disappointment to the New Zealand Government that, on & question
of particularly intimate concern to the countries of the British
aw
Commonwealth and to the other Allies of the United States, whose
sacrifices during the war and co-operation in the occupation and
control of Japan, and whose proper regard for the security of their
own peoples, entitle them to their due voice in such a matter, the
United States Government should contemplate exercising its authority
in such a way as to disregard their agreed opinion.
The New Zealnd Government understands that a proposal has
been made to the United States Government by the Government of
Australia suggesting that an expedition for purposes entirely similar
to those contemplated by the United States Government should be
despatched under the auspices of Australia or under Allied
DOB -P Unit
C

=== Page 508 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
controlled and operated by Australian or Allied persennel.
The New Zealand Government fully and warmly supports such a ps
as one of several possible means of removing New Zealand's security
objections to Japanese Antarctic whaling, while at the same time
relieving the United States of the exclusive burden of meeting the
substantial cost involved.
The grounds of the New Zealand Governmant's objeotions to
the renewal of the Japanese whaling industry in the Antarctie have
on several occasions been eaxpressed to, and are well-known by, the
Department of State, and need not be repeated now, but in case of
misunderstanding the New Zealand Governmant desires to make it
perfectly clear that its policy towards the defeated nations has
never at any time been based on the principle of reaping economic
advantage from their defeat.
The New Zealand Government, in mking
this protest against the action of the United States in this connection,
desires to emphasise that New Zealand has no economic interest in
excluding Japan from the Antarctic whaling industry beyond a general
world interest in the conservation of the whale and in the attainment
of maximm and economical returns from whaling operations.
New Zealnd Legation
Washington, D.C
June 25, 1947

CONFIDENTIAL
June 24, 1947
Memorandum of Conversation Between Dr. H. V. Evatt
and Ambassador Robert Butler
Dr. Evatt reque sted me to see him at 12:00 o'clock
noon today, and upon entering his office, he said,
"Things are not goin~ so well, are they?" My answer
was that I didn't find that they were going so bad.
He said this whaling situation had him greatly
worried and that he was not going to allow the Japanese
to fish in Australian water, and he claims that is
where they will go to fish.(He said that MacArthur
gave his orders authorizing them to fish there, stating
that he (llacArthur) got his orders from Washington.)
Dr. Evatt claims that there was an understanding between
the State Department and himself that before they gave
8
the Japanese permission to come into this area and fish
they would take it up with the Australian Government.
He said he felt that the United States was using Russian
methods and that we were just dealing with a highhanded
manner.
Dr. Evatt informed me that Australia was in a
position to handle this whaling expedition and handle
it as successfully as the Japanese. He also tried to
draw a comparison on how we vould like it if the
Japanese came into our Alaskan waters for fishing.
I informed him that I was not familiar with the
whaling situation; t hat no reports had come into our
office on same; that all we knew was what we read in
the papers.
He touched slightly on the Fourteenth Directorship
of the International Bank and Fund. He felt that the
Australians had been done an injustice. I explained it
to him for the third time, but he still feels as though
they should have been made a Director. He felt that
Italy had agreed to allow them to become one, but that
the United States had blocked it.
Regarding Manus Island he stated that he felt that
the State Department had given the Australian Government
a double-cross. All the time we were negotiating for it,
the United States Navy was dismantling the equipment.
He said that he didn't know until yesterday that the
equipment had been sold to China. He said he understood
that the Chinese could not move the equipment in the
time specified by the United States. He said that
Australia would have bought theequipment if it had been
offered to them and he felt that the United States would
use the equipment if an Agreement could have been made
that they

=== Page 532 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
that they would accept the use of Samoa and Canton
for bases. He also informed me that hè has definite
information that the State Department has offered the
use of Samoa and Canton to the United Kingdom at the
same time they were offering it to the Australian
Government. I told him that I was not aware of the
fact that they had offered Canton or Samoa to the
U.K. Dr. Evatt informed me that he had definite
information about this.
He was in a rather belligerent mood when I first
went in. He said he wanted to know when the sale was
made to the Chinese of the equipment on Manus. He
said he could find out himself, but he would appreciate
it if I could find out for him. He also seemed to be
of the opinion that he would like to have the United
States cancel the sale of the equipment to China.
I informed him that he should have his Ambassador
take up with the State Department the story on the
whaling and before I completed the conference with
him he was pretty well calmed down.
He said he was anxious to go to Japan and wanted
to know what the reason was that I wouldn't go. I
informed him that my Government felt it would look as
though we were having a prearranged conference on the
Japanese peace terms. He said, of course, this was not
the case. He thought our Government was taking an
unreasonable posit ion; that he was of the opinion that
he and I could smooth out the feeling between Atcheson
and Ball. He couldn't quite understand our reasoning.
He told me that the RAAF had offered him a Liberator,
but that he didn't want to fly in it, that he wanted a
DC-4. I suggested that possibly he could get a DC-4
from TAA.
He felt that the opposition he was receiving was
due to the fact that the Empire Conference was being
held here and that our State Department was a little
vexed about it.

=== Page 533 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to D
from Canberra, Australl
Conversation with Dr. Evatt
Source: The Sydney Morning Herald,
of Saturday, June 28, 1947,
published at Sydney, Australia
"UNILATERAL ACTION BY U.S.A."
"The growin tendency on the part of General MacArthur
and Washington to treat Japan as an American preserve,
ignoring the rights of other interested Allies, has been
distressingly demonstrated in the past week by the unilateral
action taken in respect first of whaling and now of the
phosphate industry on Anguar Island in the Palau Group.
America can no doubt argue that, since she has been granted
a strategic trusteeship over all the former Japanese
mandates, she is not answerable to the Far Eastern Commission
for her policy on Anguar. The fact, however, that the
Japanese Government has been authorised to operate the
phosphate project there surely brings the matter within
the Commission's purview. Australia, in particular, has
good reason to complain that she was not consulted on a
move which may legitimately be held to affect her security.
"However compelling America considers her reasons for
authorising a second Japanese whaling expedition, her
failure to consult, or even to notify in advance, either
the Far Eastern Commission in Washington or the Allied
Council in Tokyo cannot possibly be justified, especially
as four of her Allies had notified her that they were
strongly opposed to the project. This inexplicable
assumption of unilateral responsibility for the administra-
tion of Japan has now been sharply challenged in the Far
Eastern Commission, which is empowered 'to review, on the
request of any member, any directive issued to the supreme
conmander for the Allied Powers.' In this case, Washington
cannot take refuge in its right to issue interim directives
on matters of urgency. There has been ample time to bring
the matter before the Commission, and if, as seems likely,
it now gives a majority ruling against the whaling directive
America, as one of the foremost opponents of the abuse of
the veto to negative majority decisions in the Security
Council, can hardly invoke her own veto power.
"In the broader view, American persistence in unilateral
action in the Pacific must gravely weaken her moral case
against similar Soviet behaviour in the Balkans and elsewhere.
Washington has very properly protested strongly against the
recent Russian manoeuvres in Hungary, in defiance of inter-
national assurances and without consultation with the
western Allies. But in authorising the second Japanese
expedition to the Antarctic, America not only showed as
little apparent regard for the Far Eastern Commission as
did the Russians for the Allied Control Council in Budapest,
but, according to Dr. Evatt, she also disregarded her pledge
of prior consultation. Much of the present disagreement
probably
UNRESTRICTED

=== Page 534 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
probably stems from the strong personality of General
MacArthur. It is earnestly to be hoped the dissatisfac
tion clearly displayed in the Far Eastern Commission
will induce America to revise her policy. These
disputes are particularly distasteful to Australia, who
wishes for nothing better than to work in the closest
harmony with the United States in the Pacific."
UNRESTRICTED

Mr. Lovett stated that, as a result of the conversation
with Sir John Balfour, he felt General Hilldring should post-
pone speaking to the Secretary about the answer to the Austral-

to the proposed reply to Australia. He felt that rather than

this Government would have to supply.

that he wauld take it up with him immediately.

DATB: July 1, 1947
FROM : NA: Mr. Borton
suject: Appointment with Mr. Balfour Tuesday, July 1, 3:0o P.M.
It is understood that Mr. Balfour, Counselor of the
British Embesey, has requested an appointment today to dis-
cuss the projected SCAP-administered, Japanese-manned
Antarctic whaling expedition during the coming whaling
season. While it is not known just what points Mr. Balfour
intends to make, his general purpose is doubtless to follow
up his Government's protest of Jun 1 (attached): Following
1s the background of the matter
824.22876-1747
1) On May 4, 1947 SCAP requested permission to authorize
a whaling expedition for the 1947-48 seaeon similar to that
carried out lsst year.
2) At almost the same time notes were received from the
FN-894, 60
British, Australian and New Zealand Governments reminding this
Government of its commitment last year to consult with other
interested governments before authorizing any future Japanese
whaling operations and expressing opposition to a second
Antarctic whaling expedition. Arguments against such an
expedition included statements that Japan's participation
1n the 1947-48 expedit1on would mean a reduction in the
catches of nations party to the International Whaling
Convention; the total quantity of whale production for world
consumption would be reduced as a result of inefficient
Japanese processing operations; Japan's past whaling record
disregarded international conservation regulations; and the
Japanese whale fleet 1s a security threat.
3) These notes and SCAP's request were given careful
considerat1on in the Department and on May 27 Assistant
Secretary Hilldring discussed the matter with representatives
of the British, Australian, New Zealand and Norwegian Govern-
ments and informed them of our desire to proceed with an
expedition this year.
4) On June 9 representatives of these Governments were
handed aide-mémoires (copy of aide-mémoire to British Govern-
ment attached), setting forth our reasons for approving an
expedition, chiefly the facts that protein foods and oils from
the expedition are essential components of Japanese diet and
that the expedition would relieve the United States of
supptying these items, and that no significant security
perceived in the continued possession by Japan
of two

=== Page 538 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
of two whaling factory ships. At this meeting representatives
of all four countries expressed bitter opposition to our
proposed action and asked that their Governments be given
an opportunity to file formal protests before authorization
for the expedition was actually given.
5) The Supreme Commander, though informed of the decision
of thie Government to authorize the expedition, was requested
to take no public action in the matter until June 17, to allow
time for possible protests from the complaining Governments.
6) No formal protests were received prior to June 17 but
a paper presented to the FEC by the British and supported by
the Australian, New Zealand, Canadian and Netherlands repre-
sentatives advocated prohibition of any future Japanese whaling
during the occupation of Japan. On June 17 the British presented
the attached note requesting that the expedition be withheld or
cenceled.
7) On June 19 General McCoy announced to the FEC in a formal
state ent that the United States Government, though greatly
regretting inability to reach agreement on the paper, definitely
could not approve the British paper. Simultaneously, SCAP was
instructed to authorize the expedition.
2) Since that time, protests have been received from the
Norwegians and the Australians, the latter suggesting they
operate a Japenese whaling unit on behalf of Jepan. An aide-
mémoire, which it is ex ected that the Secretary will hand the
Australian Ambassador tomorrow or the next day, has been prepared
rejecting the Australian proposal. Our decision to authorize
the expedition, and our alleged failure to fulfill our commit-
ment to consult with the interested Governments, has evoked adverse
press comnent in Britain and Australia. A record of conversation
between Mr. Clayton and Mr. Bevin on the subject may be found in

with General Hilldring on May 27 and June 9 when this Government
presented ite aide-mémoire and that no protests were received
during that period, nor during the following week when we requested
SCAP to take no public action. No less than six weeks elapsed
between the time when it first became known in Japan that a
second whaling expedition was under consideration (a note pre-
sumably occasioned by this report was received from the British
Government on April 30) and the receipt of the Embassy's protest
of June 17.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: July 1, 1947
3:00 P.M.
SUBJECT:
Second Japanese Antarctio Whaling Expedition.
PARTICIPANTS:
Robert A. Lovett, Under Secretary of State
Sir John Balfour, Counselor, British Embassy
Harold A. Graves, Counselor, British Embassy
Hugh Borton, Chier, Division of Northeast Asian Affairs
1947 JUL

A-H
FE
NA (Mr. Fearey)
2
SEC
BC

Sir John Balfour called at his request to present a note
89%
on the subject of the second Japanese Antarctic whaling expedi-
tion objecting to the action taken by the United States Govern-
ment authorizing a second whaling expedition without what they
consider proper prior consultation and believing that the de-
cision of the whaling expedition should be left to the Far Fastern
Commission. The British note concludes that it hopes General
MacArthur's instructions can be countermanded and the question
referred back for decision to the FEC and examination of alter-
native proposals made. The Under Secretary then pointed out
that the problem seemed to divide itself into two aspects, namely,
that of procedure and that revolving around the question as to
whether or not the Japanese should be permitted to whale in the
Antarctic. On the procedural question he stated that he regretted
any difference of interpretation which might have been given to
the action of this Government on May 27 when General Hilldring
discussed the problem with representatives of the interested
countries, the handing of our aide-mémoire of June 9 to repre-
sentatives of the same countries and final instruction to SCAP
on June 16 concerning authorization of the expedition. Mr.Graves
then sveted that in discussing the question of whaling in the
Far Eastern Commission they had been lead to believe that the
Unotedtatates would present a paper on whaling for consideration
the CommiBsion. Mr. Borton then referred to an extract of
Cat..
the minutes
bu Brdo: delnicum, fu
p.le. ent
onf

=== Page 541 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
the minutes of Committee #2 of the Far Fastern Commission of
April 4, 1947 whioh stated that "The United States Member re-
ferred to the unofficial proposal made at the 48th meeting
that ir the Commission would agree to accept a fishing policy
based substantially on FEC-O35, the United States would give
favorable consideration to an FEC paper providing that whaling
beyond the area contained in SCAPIN l033 should be subject to
FEC approval." Mr. Borton added that when such a statement tas
made it was not the intention of this Government to present the
paper in question and that this may have been the cause for
some of the misunderstanding.
Mr. Lovett then outlined the difriculties confronting the
Department vis-à-vis Congress in any program which requires
expenditure of U.S. funds for assistance abroad and pointed
out that the Department would be in an untenable position if it
did not support a whaling expedition this year whereby the
Japanese would be able to provide themselves with the necessary
protein and oil without expenditure to the United States Govern-
ment.
In answer to a question as to whether enough Allied ships
were available this year for catching the total maximum 16,000
blue whale units provided for in the whaling Convention,
Mr. Graves answered that both the British and the Norwegians
had begun construction of additional factory ehips on the assump-
tion that the coneultations and Allied decisions mentioned in
the note of this Government of October 4, 1946 would mean that
no whaling expedition would take place this year. Mr. Lovett
then remarked that if the Brit1sh had acted on such an assumption
they themselves were disregarding the significance of the phrase
"Allied decision" and had themselves already decided there should
be no expedition regardless of our views in the matter. Mr.Lovett
further stated that he felt any action the British might have
taken concerning the construction of additional ships should be
considered as an ordinary business risk. So far as he could see
the issue resolved itselr into the simple question as to whether
or not the Japanese would be allowed to whale. Mr. Graves stated
that if they were allowed to whale in the place of the Japanese
they would make available to Japan its normal whale oil require-
ments, but made no mention of whale meat nor would the proceeds
from the other whale oil be available. Mr. Borton added that
such an arrangement would not make available as credit in Japan
the proceeds from the sale of the remainder of the whale oil.
Mr. Lovett concluded by saying that he saw no solution to the
problem unless agreement could be reached among the members of
the whaling Convention to allow for a catch above the 16,Oooblue
whale units which, in all probability, would be equivalent to
the aifference

=== Page 542 === (confidence 0.94)
-s
the difference between the 16,000 units and actual amount
caught last year.
Sir John Balfour thanked the Under Seoretary for his
explanation of the position of this Government and requested
that this note be given careful consideration.

=== Page 547 === (confidence 0.96)
 Hanes ly Sin

Ref.9/-/47
NOTE
His Britannic Majesty's Ambassador has the honour
to refer to his Note of the 17th June concerning the
authorisation for a second Japanese Antarctic Whaling Ex-
pedition. The decision has caused His Majesty's Govern-
ment in the United Kingdom considerable concern and Lord
Inverchapel is instructed to urge the Secretary of State
894.628/7-147
to reconsider the position, in order that there shall be
an opportunity for further discussion between the interested
Covernments, which include, in addition to that of the
United States, the Governments of Australia, New Zealand
and the United Kingdom.
2.
The desirahility or otherwise of Japanese whaling
activities, involving as it does, questions of security and
of world stocks of food and whale oil, appears to His
Majesty's Government to be essentially one in which such
consultation is called for and where unilateral action is
to be deplored. There was satisfaction that the United
States Government shared this attitude when, in their Aide
C
Memoire dated 4th October, 1946, the State Department expressed
S
complete accord with the view that long-range consideration
A
of the rights of Japan to conduct whaling operations was a
matter for Allied discussion and decision. Lord Inverchapel
was at the same time informed that no authorisation for any
08
future Japanese whaling expeditions in the Antarctic or else-
where would be given without prior consultation with the
interested Governments.
22
3.
That the United States Government, in spite of this
expression of their views and of the assurance given, should
have taken action to authorise a second expedition without
prior consultation, cannot but react adversely upon the

=== Page 548 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
principle of Allied consultations. It is also, in the
opinion of His Majesty's Government, directly damaging
to the approved machinery for Allied discussion and
decision provided by the Far Eastern Commission for the
control of Japan, since, at the time the Directive to
General MacArthur was published, a United Kingdom paper
proposing limitations upon Japanese whaling was already under
consideration in the Commission and had received the ap-
proval of a majority of the delegations at the Committee
stage. It is considered that this should have made it
apparent that interested Governments had legitimate doubts
as to the desirability of permitting the Japanese to whale,
and were entitled to have their arguments heard. Lord
Inverchapel wishes to mention, moreover, that an alternati ve
proposal by the Australian Government for the conduct of an
expedition under Australian and other Allied auspices does
not appear to have received any consideration.
4.
Mis Majesty's Government in the United Kingdom
hope therefore that the instructions to General MacArthur
can be countermanded and the question be referred back for
decision by the Far Eastern Commission and for examination
of alternative proposals.
BRITISH EMBASSY,
WASHINGTON, D. C.,
1st July, 1947.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: July 3, 1947
2:30 P.M.
SUBJECT:
Second Antarctic Whaling Expedition.
PARTICIPANTS: Norman J.O. Makin, Ambassador of Australia
Alfred Stirling, Minister of Australia
The Secretary

Hugh Borton, Chief, Division of Northeast Asian Affairs

Ambassador Makin called at his request to express further
the views of his Government in reference to the proposed second
SCAP-sponsored Antarctic whaling expedition. Ambassador Makin
requested that he be allowed to read some notes which he had
made in order that he could express more clearly the thought
of his Government and people in reference to the whaling expe-
dition. Ambassador Makin then read the attached, adding only
that Mr. Menzies, the minority leader in Parliament,had recently
strongly supported Mr. Evatt's stand on whaling so that the
Australian people took a united stand on the matter.
894.628/7-
The Secretary replied that he was not prepared to answer
Ambassador Makin today but that he would take his points into
serious consideration.
The conversation terminated at 2:39 P.M.

=== Page 559 === (confidence 0.97)
AUSTRALIA
3rd July, 1947.
My Government have asked me to convey to you the
very deep concern which they themselves feel and which is shared
by the Australian people as a whole on the proposal of a further
whaling expedition from Japan.
We feel that the decision to permit a second
expedition to the Antarctic is wholly unwarranted in vlew of
the offer which Australia has made to accept responsibility,
either herself or in association with other interested nations,
to undertake an expedition and provide the result of the catoh
for ultimate distribution in Japan by the Supreme Commander.
As regards expense we feel that this can easily be
arranged to our mutual satisfaction so as to avoid any further
burden upon the American taxpayer.
894.628/7-347
Furthermore we believe that the necessary arrangements
can be made without involving any delay.
Our methods of whaling will undoubtedly guarantee a
greater supply of oil from the catch of whales allowed.
We are not asking for the exclusion of the Japanese as
P
a whole.
There is the northern Pacific nearer their own
country.
I draw your attention to the convertibility of these
Japanese whaling ships for naval use. What concerns us is the
potential threat to the security of our country. In the last
15 years the presence of Japanese ships and in particular the
pearling fleets in the neighbourhood of Australia has been synonymous
DC/R
A160
jev------
Cut

=== Page 560 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
with spying.
With the memory of the Japanese invasion of New Guinea
and Timor, and the destruction of our northern port of Darwin
fresh in mind, the Australian people can only regard the presence
of Japanese ships whether whalers or pearlers or otherwise in the
seas adjacent to Australia with the deepest resentment and appre-
hension.
A second visit of Japanese ships and Japanese crews
following on thet of last year will give great encouragement to
the Japanese in a direction which we can only regard as most
undesirable..
I would emphasise our concern that an enemy country
should be considered in this way contrary to the interests of an
Ally. We have suffered the treachery of this enemy as you have,
and none understand that treachery better than the United States.
My people are deeply concerned because this question
of the expedition appeared to have been settled unilaterally at the
very moment when the matter was coming before the FEC.
My Government hope that the Government of the United
States will be prepared to review this matter which from our point
of view is one of major importance and which has deeply stirred
the whole Australian people.

Four. Australian contention that Australian people
are up in arms is not accepted here. Not only have
some Australian papers stated editorially that project
is sound but local Australian ofricials other than
MacMahon Ball have privately characterized Australian
politicians sound and fury as tempest in teapot. It
is the opinion here of those closely familiar with
Australian political scene, that Australian protests
flov from polioy of endeavoring by loud assertion to.
bring Australia to place of effective predominance in
the Far East. This in turn stems from internal
Australian politics and political maneuvers of
Australian ofricials who have made so much ill-
considered public slamor in this matter. In the eyes
of the Japanese people and other peoples in the Far
East the carrying through of the second expedition
has been blown up by the Australians into a matter
of the prestige of the US and of SCAP: that is, having
made a decision that is entirely just, humanitarian
and practical, whether US and SCAP can or cannot be
overridden by the unreasonable domands of officials
of a small nation which desires Jap whaling vessels fcr
own venal commercial interests. (It will be recalled
that Australian minister in China went so far as to
issue statement to the Chinese people in the matter.)
Five. Question
SECRET

=== Page 564 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-4-#183, July 5, 8 p.m., from Tokyo
Five Question of security interlaced with
referenoes to past Jap treachery et cetera which the
australians seem to have adopted as emotional
foundation of their complaints is one which does
not aotually exist. It was correctly stated in
this headquarters press release 22 June: "As
SCAP-controlled Japanese-manned ships operating to
repatriate 5 million Japanese prisoners of war
and civilians have during the past 18 months entered
aumerous Allied ports in the West and Southvest
raciric without incident, it is believed that no
problem of security is involved in this second whaling
expedition." Sydney was one of such ports. Further-
more, while the Antarctic Ocean 1s not an Australian
lake, appeasement of the Australians in this particular
matter will undoubtedly support them in making faotual
their implied contention that freedom of the seas and
use by others, of those international waters is subject
to Australian approval.
Six. Instructions to the Japanese Government
in the matter have already been givon and active
preparations for the expedition have begun. The
Jap Government and newly elected Diet have tendered
formal expressions of gratitude for assistance to
their problems which has been provided by the
authorization. Due to 1ts importance in the food
deficit, authorization of the expedition has been
featured most prominently by Japanese press and has
provided very desirable and needed boost to
Japanese morale to help carry them through the critical
food situation. Reversal would have exceedingly
unfortunate repercussions.
Seven. There seems to us to be involyed also a simple
question of right and wrong. This is the question
whether we should uphold a decision that 1s based
entirely on considerations of right. I bave in
mind not only the merits of the matter, which have
been previously discussed, but also the provisions
of the Potsdam Declaration, notably:
(Eleven) Japan
SECRET

=== Page 565 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-5-#183, July 5, 8 p.m., from Tokyo
"(Eleven) Japan shall be permitted to maintain such
industries as will sustain her economy and permit the
execution of just reparations in kind, but not
those which would enable her to re-arm for war.
To this end, access to, as distinguished from control
of, raw materials shali be permitted. Eventual
Japanese prticipation in world trade relations shall
be permitted."
This principle was recently reaffirmed by Far
Eastern Commission in its basic-post-surrender policy
for Japan:
One. Too. D. Japan shall be permitted to maintain
such industries as will sustain her economy and permit
the exaction of just reparations in kind, but not
those which would enable her to rearm for war. To
this end access to, as distinguished from control
of, raw materials should ba permitted. Eventual
Japanese participation in world trade relations
will be permitted."
The foregoing also follows the fourth basio principle
laid down in the Atlantic Charter as follows:
"They will endeavor, with due respect for their
oxisting obligations, to further the en joyment by
all states, great or small, victor or vanquished,
of access, cn equal terms, to the trede and to the
raw materials of the world which are needed for their
economic prosperity." This principle is also one
of those incorporated in the Declaration by the
Caited Nations.
Eight. Any compromise of these principles cr. any
appeasement of the Australians will without question
seriously undermined American prestige in this part
of the world. Appeasement will have most adverse
effects on the occupation which has been so successfully
carried forward by the prestige of the United States.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
FOR THE PRESS
JULY 2, 1947
NO. 545
ANGUAR ISLAND PHOSPHATE INDUSTRY
At his press conference today, Secretary Marshall
made the following statement:
"The Australian Goverrment on May 29 expressed its
concern over a reported decision by the Supreme Commander
to transfer responsibility for the managenent of the
Anguar phosphate industry to the Japanese laborers in
an area close to Australian fisheries. In fact the
operation is, and will remain, under the close supervision
of the Supreme Commander:s Headquarters. All Japanese
will be returned to Japan on completion of the project,
which is designated to alleviate the fertilizer shortage
in Japan and so reduce the burden of Japanese food supplies
on the United States. The Supreme Commander has been
informed of the Australian Covernment's concern and, in
answer to cur request, has provided full information in
the matter."

DATE: July 18, 1961
FROM : NA - Mrs. Dunning
sUECT: Discussion between General McCoy and
Ambassador Makin at the meeting of the
Far Eastern Commission on July 17, 1947.
The highpoint of this meeting was the verbal
battle between the United States representative on
the Far Eastern Commission, General McCoy and the
Australian representative, Ambassador Makin over
the question of the FEC paper on Japanese whaling.
General McCoy suggested that consideration or the
paper be postponed in view of the continuing
diplomatio exchanges on the subject. At this point,
Ambassador Makin stated that a vote should be taken
as soon as possible on the paper since diplomatio
negotiations were at an end.  General McCoy took
exception to this statement and stated that he did
not like the insinuation on the part of the Aus
tralian Ambassador that the Us Government was de-
894.628
liberately procrastinating on this question. Mr.
Makin stated that he did not see how any such
inferences could be drawn from his statement.
General McCoy referred him to previous statements
in the record and stated that in view of the fact
that diplomatic exchanges were continuing with Nor-
way, the United Kingdom and other countries con-
cerned, that it was dishonest to accuse the Us of
deliberate procrastination. Hr. Makin stated that
he was making a formal protest to the Commission
7-1847
concerning General McCoy's accusations. General
McCoy then rererred Mr. Makin to the Australian
Premier's statement of last week that the discussion
about the question of Japanese whaling was making a
mouhtain out of a molehill.

in the premises. There is enclosed one copy of a report

(Dr.Deason)

8

The Natural Resowreem Section of this Headquarters has
9
commented upon this repert as follows:
2
"The basio data referred to by Mr. Cooman in
subject report are substantially in agreement with the
report submitted by Lt. McCracken, Natural Resources
Section, SCAP representative on the 1946-47 whaling
expedition to the Antarctic. In several parts of the
2
report, however, the interpretation and statements made
5,9
by Mr. Coonan tend to give a misleading impression of
some of the operations during the expedition.
f
"Reference fifth and sixth paragraphs, page 4,
subject report. The impression is that the complete
backbone and both sides of the ribs wero frequently
discarded. Actually, detailed records show bones were
discarded on only eighteen days and that the total loss of
oil from suoh discarding was less than 80 metric tons, com-
pared to a total production of 12,178 metric tons for both
factory ships. These records also show that the discarding
of this quantity of bones made it possible to process
additional whales which yielded approximately 720 metric
tons of meat and 260 metric tons of oil. By this action
met gains of 180 metric tons of oil and 720 metric tons
of meat were realized, with no loss of whale oil.
"Reference page 20, subject report, it is stated
that 2,s52 tons ef mhale bone were disoarded, resulting
in the loss of 1,326 tons of oil. This figure differe
greatly

=== Page 577 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokye's No. 1186
July 25, 1949.
-2-
greatly from the figure of 80 metric tons of oil lost,
as stated in precoding paragraph. The figures shom on
page 20 are derived figures, based on the assumptiem that
the percentage of oil recovered frem whale bones is 50
percent. The actual extrastion figure obtained by the
Japanese on the expedition is much below the figure given
by Mr. Coonan, as indicated by the records of the expedi-
tion.
"Reference eighth paragraph, page 5, subject report,
which states that 7,000 tons of whale produots were gaimed
by discarding bones. This figure is based on a etatement
olaimed to have been made by the manager. Figures obtained
by the SCaP representative from the whaling company. how-
ever, indicated that the gains were approximately l80 metrioc
tons of oil and 720 metric tons of meat.
"Reference eighth paragraph, page 4. It is claimed
that oil production per blue whale unit was 9.5? tons, as
compared to 17-19 tons for pre-war expeditions. This
comparison is not a valid one, since 2,949 tons of blubber
were shipped to Japan for consumption. Considering this
blubber in adjusting the production of oil, a yield of
14.6 tons of oil for blue whalo unit is obtained. A total
production per blue whale unit of oil, meet and other
products was 37 metric tons. This figure indicates a
much higher utilization of the whales than that for any
pre-war expedition. This discussion in this paragraph
applies to the Hashidate-Maru fleet, where Mr. Coonan
was an observer. The figure of 2,949 tons of blubber
is considerably less than that shown in the subject
report since in that report were included considerable
quantities of products whieh have very lew oil content
and are therefore not ordinarily processed. Hewever, in
the subject report, the figure for oil is considerably
too low when compared to the amount of blubber obtained,
so that the cstimated yield fram products shipped to Japan
is approximately the same as that calculated by the SCAP
observers record. The Japanese estimate that under best
conditions, the amount of oil they obtained per blue whale
unit would be approximately that obtained on Nissin-Maru
on the 1946-47 oxpedition -- 17.5 tons. This figure 1s
considerably less than the 19-20 tons reported for
Norwegian expeditions. If the Japanese processed all
meat as well as all blubber they would obtain a wnit of
approximately 19.4 tons, w ich figure is oqual to the
Norwegian figure. No precise comperison is possible
without taking into account: (1) the month during
which operations were carried on, and (2) the sise of
the whales captured, since late season operations and
large whales will result in a higher yield of blue whale
units.
Reference

Takye's No.11ee
July 25, 1947
-3
"Raference seventh paragraph, page 5, which sives
the impression that the Japanese are not comcermed about
eil productiom. This inference is incerrect because
the whale oil is zeeded in Japan as well as elsewhere,
but diversien of comsiderable propertiem of the expeditiom's
productiom was approved hecause of the critical meed of
other countries and the high value of whale eil. In plase
of the whale oil, a muoh greater amount or cheaper oil
can be imported to supply Japanese noeds.
"Reference page six, "reference liver", sugesting
that the liver was mot used. Actually, it was salted and
brought iato Japan for further processing ashore. Remder-
ing aboard ship is preferable but not essential. Due to
the shertage of time available for outritting the vessel,
it was impessible to assemble and install adequate liver
processing equipment.
"Reference nart to the last paragraph, page so.
indicating the belief that the only purpose of a whaling
expedition is to obtain whale oil. This view appears to
be extremely biased and one-sided, since direct consumption
of meat and blubber will result in better utilization than
immediate processing, discarding the residue, and conversion
of the oil lato various food items.
"Reference conclusions, page 23. A nuabcr of
contradictory statements are noted:
a. In the first paragraph, it is stated that only
ordinary skill and intelligence are required for men in
key positions on an antarotic whaling expedition.
Mr. Coonan states that he had no difficulty in scoring
a hit with the harpoon gun, but he also adaitted that
the whale had already been made fast by the first harpoon
line.
b. In the second paragraph, he remarks that there
is nothing umique about the position of a whaling fleet
master, mxeept thet "it requires a therough kmowlege of
the Antarctic and ice conditions". shether such knowledge
makes a man in any way unique is a matter of opinion.
c. In the third paragraph, he states that the
manager must have experience in all phases of whaling
industry, including catchers, factory equipment, and
flensing. It appears that such skill and knowledge could
be considered ordinary.
d. Natural Resources Section agrees that the
Australians could be trained to undertake all operatiens
involved in an Antarotic Expedition. However, to reach
a level of average efficiency compared to other natiens
which

=== Page 579 === (confidence 0.98)
Tekye's No. 1188
July 25, 1947.
which have long participated in Antaretic whaling, might
reasonably require at least several years' traiming on
the part of the Australians. The initial partieipatiem
of the Australians in the proposed 1947-48 expeditiom
could not be expected to be at a relatively high level
of efficiency because it would be the first year of
operations for them; hence production might pessibly be
below that which could be acoomplished by an expeditien
completely manned by Japanese with the advantage of 8 years'
experience in Antarctio whaling. Any decrease in potential
production would be, of course, highly objectionable to
the mamy interests involved, especially at a time when the
food and oil requirements of Japan and other nations require
the maximum production from the most efrielent utilization
of equipment and menpowor."

Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Lovett:
WHALE OIL TRANSPORT FROM JAPAN TO GERMANY
In accord with your request during telephone conver-
sation yesterday morning, the situation, based on informa-
tion from the Office of Chief of Transporation, War De-
x~
partment, is recited as follows:
894.85
The U. S. military Governments of Germany and Japan
have arranged for the transfer of 71o0 tons of whale oil
now in Japan and urgently needed in Germany for edible
food products. The military Government of Japan (SCAP)
has a moderate size suitable whaling tanker under SCAJAP
flag and Japanese crew ready in Japan for immediate loading
of the whale oil. General MacArthur's headquarters propose
the use of this vessel for transport of the whale oil. If
not so used, the vessel will be idle until her next whaling
expedition in November.
Approval for whale oil transport was denied by the
ar Department because of State Department disapproval on
grounds that operation of a SCAJAP flag vessel with Japanese
crew to areas other than nearby Far East areas might create
problems or unfavorable criticism and reaction in some
quarters.
C
Efforts during the last two months to obtain a medium
S
size clean tanker under any flag (except SCAJAP) have re-
A/
vealed that no such vessel is or will be available in Far
Eastern waters.
The only other alternative would be the use of a
16,O00 ton Maritime Commission tanker regularly employed
68
in carrying fuel oil. Such a tanker, the American SS
"COXCOMB HILL", is due at Guam August 7 for discharge.
f
The agents, operating the vessel for Maritime Commission
9
account, state that if the Army must use the vessel for
whale oil, the decision by Army and ANPB must be made by
2
8
Monday, July 28.
Peps
16.

=== Page 582 === (confidence 0.98)
GO BEAVER STREET
NEW YORK 4,N.Y.
Mr. Robert A. Lovett
-2-
July 26, 1947
If used for whale oil, this tanker would require to ballast
to Japan and be cleaned very carefully to avoid contamina-
tion of the whale oil. Not only would considerable time be
required for cleaning, but doubts are expressed that such
cleaning would ensure non-contamination. Although the
7l00 tons of whale oil would occupy only one half her
cargo capacity on the long voyage to Germany, full freight
on a full cargo would be payable - more than $300,000.
There is a worldwide shortage of commissioned tankers
for current fuel requirements. The use of the "COXCOMB
HILL" instead of the SCAJAP whaling tanker makes no prac-
tical shipping sense, nor any economic sense. It therefore
appears that justification would require to be based upon
the highest grounds of political policy.
It has been suggested that several Army officers
placed aboard the SCAJAP whaling tanker during the v oyage
could at any scheduledor emergency time or port clarify
the status of the vessel with any authorities and explain
the special purpose of the voyage, the itinerary of which
could be detailed prior to commencement.
Prompt action to preclude a possible error appears
pressing.
Very truly yours,
Dueley B. Donald
DBD/o
Dudley B. Donald

=== Page 583 === (confidence 0.95)
TO
: U
- Mr. Lovett:
DATE:
July 31, 19
FROM
SUBJECT:
Mr. Wood passed the atteched letter on to me and
I have been working on this matter and wish to report
that some progress is being made although it is difficult
to ascertain a number of the facts.
The State Department did not actually disapprove
the operation of a SCAJAP flag vessel as indicated in
the letter but rather told the War Department that in
view of the problems concerned, the matter should
be cleared with the British at least before they went
ahead. The War Department is holding up on this
pending the outcome of discussions I have had with the
British in cooperation with FE. The British Shipping
Attaché agrees that the use of a T-2 tanker for this
purpose would be unjustified at the present time in view
of the critical world petroleum situation and the
shortage of tankers.
A third alternative which may be satisfactory to
894.628
both the British and the Wer Department and which would
avoid possible adverse repercussions would be to ship
the whale oil in deep tanks in foreign cargo liners.
I shall report further developments as they occur.
7-2647
Attachment:

from Dudley B.Donald.

PARTICIPANTS: Dr. Deason, Fish and Wildlif• Service, Department of the Interior
IR - Mr. Castleman - Fisheries and Wiidlife Branch.
COPIES TO:

1-1400
In a telephone conversation with Dr. Deason on Angust 20, 1947 I
asked if he would mind mentioning a couple of points of interest to
us in his reply to the personal letter he received from Mr. Herrington.
I suggested that he might, with regard to the extension of Japanese
fishing areas discussed in Herrington''s letter, mention the fact that
the matter of a fishery policy for the Mandated islands is under
CS/V
consideration here and that something definite should be forthcoming
soon.
I also suggested that be might ask Herrington for a further
explanation of his point 2 "Placing a lid on further expansion of the
Japanese fishing fleet" explaining that this is a matter of great interest
in view of its possible implications with regard to the Peace Treaty and
post occupation policy.
I asked Dr. Deason if, since this was personal correspondence, it
might not be better for him to include these ponts as part of his own
reply. He said that he was also interested in these points and that he
would be pleased to include them in his reply. He said that he is going
to the Phillipines soon (Ootoberl) and that either Herrington might come
to Manila or that if this was not possible he might go to Tokyo for a
short visit.
MAY 12 1948

DATB: 8/18/47

FROM : Mr. Clancy
suject: Telephone Call from Mr. Cross, Shipping Attaché, British
Embassy.
Mr. Cross called with regard to the sale of the C-1 Mv-1.
He had a rather long story to tell of which the following arethe
main points:
1) Under the Land Leathers Agreement the British had an option
to purchase the C-1 MAV-1on lend-lease to them if they made ap-
plication to the Maritime Commission by March 27, 1947. However,
they failed to take advantage of this option except for one ship
but filed late applications after March 27 for an additional three.
2) These three ships were due for redelivery to the United
States in June and are now actually in this country. However, in
view of the British application for their purchase it was agreed
that the crews would be maintained and the ships actually stay in
British hands until the application is approved by the Maritime
Commission.
3) The Commission stated that they could not approve theso
applications without reexamination of the whole C-1 MAv-1 picture
and requested a report on the outstanding applications for these
ships. In the meantime the British are having considerable trouble
with the Lascar crews which they have aboard these ships and whose
service contracts are running out.
4) Mr. Cross stated that he believed that it would be helpful
S
if we used our good offices to assure that these three ships were
in view of the Ship Sales Acty section charging the Maritime Com-
mission to take in account wartime losses in allocating desir-
FW
able ships for sale abroad, it seemed rather peculiar that Finland
should take precedence over the U.K.
I made no comments on what he had to say but told him that I
would check with the Commission to determine what action the Commis-
sion is taking on the matter and would call him early next week.

for me to inquire whether the Ministry of Transport could
arrange for this whale oil to be carried in the deep tanks
of cargo liners. I am pleased to say that British lines
can arrange to lift approximately 3,500 tons during the
period September through November and in addition, can lift
the balance during the period December 1947 to February 1948
London inform me that they have consulted the Food and
Agriculture Division, Berlin, who are agreeable to phasing
the reception of the whale oil over the periods referred to.
In the light of our various conversations on this
subject, may I assume please that we can tell London that
they can go ahead with the necessary and detailed arrang
FILHD
ments.

=== Page 609 === (confidence 0.96)
TO
: A-H - Mr. Lewis
DATE:
August 26, 1947
PROM :
OE - Mr. Whitman
SUBJECT:
FEC action on fishing policy
We are attempting to find a compromise formula on
restriction of the Japanese fishing area which will
attain majority support and put the Australians and the
USSR, who are advocating sole FEC jurisdiction over any
extension of the fishing area, in the minority.
8
One suggestion which has been explored is to define
b
"near" which is the crucial word in the original U. S.
f
paper: "Japanese fishing should not be permitted near
6
areas under Allied jurisdiction without prior permission
て
from the country concerned". At one time the U. S.
tentatively suggested that "near" be defined as 50 miles.
A
The Netherlands has made a counter proposal of 150 miles.
Substantively this makes no change in SCAP's situation
since it is recognized that by any interpretation of the
present policy no additional valuable fishing grounds
can be attained without consultation. However, IR now
objects to stating a specific figure because it might
become a precedent as a definition of a security area
and return to plague us in treaty negotiations. As an
alternative method of assuring any country that SCAP will
not unilaterally effect their security intorests by
extending the area, IR has suggested that the policy
provide that:
"Any country concerned, may, if in its opinion
security considerations warrant, inform the Supreme
Commander of its interpretation of the word 'near',
as employed in the above-mentioned paragraph of
FEC-035, with regard to its own coasts.
"If any differences exist between the Supreme
Commander and any country with regard to such
country"s interpretation of 'near', discussions
GkR
should be held between the Supreme Commemder and
such country with a view to reaching agreement.
Until agreement is reached the Supreme Commander
shall continue to operate under the provisions of
paragraph (b) Policy Directive FEC-O35 as heretofore."
If you see no objection to this proposal I will
proceed to sound out other countries as to its acceptability.
CC: NA
CAD - Col. Fahey.
OE:RHWhitman:emh

=== Page 618 === (confidence 0.96)
TO
7ile
DATE: September 16,
FROm : OE - Mr. hitman
SUBJECT: Cable to POLAD re Japanese fishers.
The attached cable has been prepared by JK and IR. Thaoconsiderations
are as follows:
94.628/9-447
1. The nearness of the Japanese peace treaty discussions, where
Japanese fisheries will be a major topic, suggests there should be a
minimum of international controversy at that time with regard to Japanese
fishing.
2. There has been considerable pressure at the FEC for a revision of
policy so as to contain Japanese fishing within present boundaries. Such
a revision would reduce the discretion of SCAP. It is desirable that
this policy revision not come to a vote by the FEC. Proposals mmde by
SCAP and rejected by USSR might stimulate policy revision pressure.
Expreience thus far suggests the USSR to uld probably reject SCAP proposals.
3. Off-Kamchatka fishing rights for Japan would probably mean more
salmon imports into the U.S. By itself, this consideration is not con-
clusive. It is, however, a point to be considered when judging various
factors in this situation and the possible advantages of SCAP making
proposals.
4. If Sebald has reason to believe the USSR would accede to Off-
Kamchatka proposals, and if the Japanese are prepared to take full
advantage of agreements expanding the fishing area, then it may be
desirable for SCAP to open negotiations. The cable so provides. It asks,
CS/A
in such event, that the Department receive more information before con-
clusion of negotiations.
30

FW.
